(t,x_{0},\mu)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
.
By item (ii) in the statement, we know that
equation (
2.4
)
is already assumed to have at least one strong solution, and this for any
starting point in
(t,x_{0},\mu)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
.
By a straightforward modification of the Yamada-Watanabe theorem, we deduce that
the state equation
(
2.4
)
is uniquely strongly solvable for any starting point in
(t,x_{0},\mu)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
. And then,
by
adapting
the proof of

[
20
, Proposition 1.31]
(which is itself
inspired from the remark below
[
56
, Theorem 6.2.2]
),
strong uniqueness implies that the
solution to
(
2.4
)
forms a strong Markov process. Then,
following once again

[
38
, Proposition 3.2 & Theorem 3.4]
, we can find a new
Borel function

\varphi^{0}:[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\otimes{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
such that, for any
s\in[0,T]
,

(2.22)

\begin{split}&{\mathbb{P}}^{0}\Bigl(\bigl\{Z_{s}^{0}=\varphi^{0}(s,X_{s}^{0},\mu_{s})\bigr\}\Bigr)=1.\end{split}

Recalling the assumption
(
2.5
), it is quite easy to prove that

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}|Y_{t}^{0}|\in L^{\infty}(\Omega^{0},{\mathbb{P}}^{0})
. And then,
expanding
(|Y_{t}^{0}|^{2})_{0\leq t\leq T}
by means of Itô’s formula,
we obtain
\|\int_{0}^{\cdot}Z_{t}^{0}\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{0}\|_{\textrm{\rm BMO}}<\infty
, and
then

\|\int_{0}^{\cdot}\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{0}\|_{\textrm{\rm BMO}}<\infty
.

We then define the tilted noise

(2.23)

\tilde{B}_{t}^{0}:=B_{t}^{0}+\sigma_{0}^{-1}\Bigl(\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})+\alpha^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t,\quad t\in[0,T].

We justify in the fourth step below
that
we can apply Girsanov theorem
to prove that

\tilde{\bm{B}}^{0}

is a Brownian motion under the probability measure

(2.24)

\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{{\mathbb{P}}}_{(\alpha^{0},\alpha)}^{0}}{\mathrm{d}{\mathbb{P}}_{(\alpha^{0},\alpha)}^{0}}={\mathscr{E}}_{T}\biggl(-\sigma_{0}^{-1}\int_{0}^{\cdot}\Bigl(\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})+\alpha^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})\Bigr)\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{0}\biggr).

And we rewrite the BSDE in (
2.21
) as

(2.25)

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}Y_{t}^{0}=-\Bigl(f_{t}^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})+L^{0}\bigl(X_{t}^{0},\alpha^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})\bigr)\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t-\Bigl(\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})+\alpha^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})\Bigr)\cdot Z_{t}^{0}\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 30.0pt+\sigma_{0}Z_{t}^{0}\cdot\mathrm{d}\tilde{B}_{t}^{0},\quad t\in[0,T].\end{split}

As before, we observe from the (strict) convexity property of
L^{0}
that

\begin{split}&L^{0}\bigl(X_{t}^{0},\alpha^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})\bigr)+\Bigl(\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})+\alpha^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})\Bigr)\cdot Z_{t}^{0}\geq L^{0}\bigl(X_{t}^{0},-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})\bigr),\end{split}

with equality if and only if
\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})+\alpha^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})=0
.
We then have (the BMO condition, which can be transferred from the original probability measure

{\mathbb{P}}_{(\alpha^{0},\alpha)}^{0}

to the new probability measure

\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{(\alpha^{0},\alpha)}^{0}

–see Theorem
[
40
, Theorems 2.3 and 3.6]
–,
makes it possible to take expectation in
(
2.25
))

(2.26)

J^{0}_{\textrm{\rm w}}({\alpha}^{0},\alpha)=Y_{0}^{0}\geq\tilde{\mathbb{E}}^{0}_{(\alpha^{0},\alpha)}\biggl[g^{0}(X_{T}^{0},\mu_{T})+\int_{0}^{T}\Bigl(f_{t}(X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})+L^{0}\bigl(X_{t}^{0},-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})\bigr)\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t\biggr].

Under
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{(\alpha^{0},\alpha)}^{0}
,

({\bm{X}}^{0},{\bm{\mu}},\tilde{\bm{B}}^{0})=(X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t},\tilde{B}^{0}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
solves the system

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{0}=-\nabla_{p}H^{0}\bigl(X_{t}^{0},\varphi^{0}(s,X_{s}^{0},\mu_{s})\bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}\tilde{B}_{t}^{0},\\
&\partial_{t}\mu_{t}=\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x_{0}}\mu_{t}-\textrm{\rm div}_{x_{0}}\bigl(\alpha(t,X_{t}^{0},\cdot,\mu_{t})\mu_{t}\bigr),\quad t\in[0,T].\end{split}

Here, we observe that

\|\int_{0}^{\cdot}\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},-\nabla_{p}H^{0}\bigl(X_{t}^{0},\varphi^{0}(s,X_{s}^{0},\mu_{s}))\cdot\mathrm{d}\tilde{B}_{t}^{0}\|_{\textrm{\rm BMO}}=\|\int_{0}^{\cdot}\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})\cdot\mathrm{d}\tilde{B}_{t}^{0}\|_{\textrm{\rm BMO}}<\infty

(again, this follows from
Theorem
[
40
, Theorem 3.6]
).
This provides one weak solution to the state equation
(
2.4
), driven by

\beta^{0}(t,x_{0},\mu):=-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(x_{0},\varphi^{0}(t,x_{0},\mu))
,
that satisfies the BMO condition
(
2.5
).
By Lemma

2.9
, this
weak solution is necessarily unique and, therefore, the pair

(\beta^{0},\alpha)
is
admissible in the sense of Definition

2.2
. Then, the right-hand side in
(
2.26
)
coincides
with
J^{0}_{\textrm{\rm w}}(\beta^{0},\alpha)
,

By item 2 in
Definition
2.7
,
the inequality in
(
2.26
)
must become an equality and then, for almost every
(t,\omega^{0})\in[0,T]\times\Omega^{0}
under the measure

\textrm{\rm Leb}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
, it holds

\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})+\alpha^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})=0
, which proves that

({\bm{X}}^{0},{\bm{\mu}})
coincides with the solution
of
(
2.11
)–(
2.12
).

Fourth Step.

We now justify the application of Girsanov theorem in
(
2.17
),
(
2.18
)
and
(
2.19
).
We start from
(
2.4
)
for an admissible pair
(\alpha^{0},\alpha)
as in the statement of Definition

2.2
, with

\alpha
satisfying
(
2.9
)
(which is the case in
(
2.15
)
because of
(
2.13
)).
Then, Lemma
2.9
says that the BMO condition
(
2.10
) is satisfied under

\bar{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
. This observation applies here
to both

\alpha^{0}

as in (
2.15
) and
\alpha^{0}\equiv\beta^{0}
(with

\beta^{0}
as in (
2.17
)).
In particular, choosing now

\alpha^{0}
as in
(
2.15
),
the process
(\int_{[0,t]}[\beta^{0}(s,X_{s}^{0},\mu_{s})-\alpha^{0}(s,X_{s}^{0},\mu_{s})]\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{s}^{0})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is
BMO under
\bar{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
. Let now

\check{\mathbb{P}}^{0}:={\mathscr{E}}_{T}\biggl(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\alpha^{0}(s,X_{s}^{0},\mu_{s})\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{s}^{0}\biggr)\cdot\overline{\mathbb{P}}^{0}.

By
[
40
, Theorem 3.6]
, we know that

(\int_{[0,t]}[\beta^{0}(s,X_{s}^{0},\mu_{s})-\alpha^{0}(s,X_{s}^{0},\mu_{s})]\cdot\mathrm{d}\check{B}_{s}^{0})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is BMO under

\check{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
, where

\check{\bm{B}}^{0}=(\check{B}^{0}_{t}:=B_{t}^{0}-\int_{0}^{t}\alpha^{0}(s,X_{s}^{0},\mu_{s})\mathrm{d}s)_{0\leq t\leq T}

is a Brownian motion under
\check{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
.
Since
\check{\mathbb{P}}^{0}\circ({\bm{X}}^{0},{\bm{\mu}},\check{\bm{B}}^{0})^{-1}={\mathbb{P}}^{0}_{(\alpha^{0},\alpha)}
,
this shows that
(\int_{[0,t]}[\beta^{0}(s,X_{s}^{0},\mu_{s})-\alpha^{0}(s,X_{s}^{0},\mu_{s})]\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{s}^{0})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is BMO under

{\mathbb{P}}_{(\alpha^{0},\alpha)}^{0}
, which suffices to apply Girsanov theorem
in
(
2.17
),
(
2.18
)
and
(
2.19
).

We now proceed in a similar manner to
justify the Girsanov transformation in
(
2.23
) and
(
2.24
).
In fact, it suffices to apply the same argument as above but with
\alpha^{0}

as in the third step, see (
2.21
),
and with
\beta^{0}(t,x_{0},\mu)=-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(x_{0},\varphi^{0}(t,x_{0},\mu))
, see (
2.22
).
∎

2.6
System of master equations

Following the analysis performed in

[
13
]
, we
associate with the Major/Minor MFG
a system of master equations. Formally, it reads as a pair of two equations for the value
V^{0}
to the major player
and the value
V
to the minor player.

The equation for
V^{0}
reads

(2.27)

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}V^{0}(t,x_{0},\mu)+\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_{0}^{2}\Delta_{x_{0}}V^{0}(t,x_{0},\mu)-H^{0}\bigl(x_{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}V^{0}(t,x_{0},\mu)\bigr)+f_{t}^{0}(x_{0},\mu)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\Bigl\{\tfrac{1}{2}{\rm div}_{y}(\partial_{\mu}V^{0}(t,x_{0},\mu,y))-\partial_{\mu}V^{0}(t,x_{0},\mu,y)\cdot\nabla_{p}H\bigl(y,\nabla_{x}V(t,x_{0},y,\mu)\bigr)\Bigr\}\mathrm{d}\mu(y)=0,\\
&V^{0}(T,x_{0},\mu)=g^{0}(x_{0},\mu),\end{split}

for
(t,x_{0},\mu)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
.

The equation for
V
is

(2.28)

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}V(t,x_{0},x,\mu)+\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}V(t,x_{0},x,\mu)+\tfrac{1}{2}{\sigma_{0}^{2}}\Delta_{x_{0}}V(t,x_{0},x,\mu)-H\bigl(x_{0},x,\nabla_{x}V(t,x_{0},x,\mu)\bigr)+f_{t}(x_{0},x,\mu)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-\nabla_{p}H^{0}\big(x_{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}V^{0}(t,x_{0},\mu)\big)\cdot\nabla_{x_{0}}V(t,x_{0},x,\mu)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\Bigl\{\tfrac{1}{2}{\rm div}_{y}(\partial_{\mu}V(t,x_{0},x,\mu,y))-\partial_{\mu}V(t,x_{0},x,\mu,y)\cdot\nabla_{p}H\bigl(y,\nabla_{x}V(t,x_{0},y,\mu)\bigr)\Bigr\}\mathrm{d}\mu(y)=0,\\
&V(T,x_{0},x,\mu)=g(x_{0},x,\mu),\end{split}

for
(t,x_{0},x,\mu)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
.

The following statement clarifies the connection between
(
2.27
)–(
2.28
)
and
(
2.11
)–(
2.12
).

Proposition 2.15
.

Assume that the master equation
(
2.27
)–(
2.28
)
has a classical solution
(V^{0},V)
in the sense that

1.

(t,x_{0},\mu)\mapsto(\partial_{t}V^{0}(t,x_{0},\mu),\nabla_{x_{0}}V^{0}(t,x_{0},\mu),\nabla^{2}_{x_{0}}V^{0}(t,x_{0},\mu))
is continuous on
[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
(with the latter
factor being equipped with any distance metricizing weak convergence on
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
, for instance
{\mathbb{W}}_{1}
);

(t,x_{0},\mu,y)\mapsto(\partial_{\mu}V^{0}(t,x_{0},\mu,y),\nabla_{y}\partial_{\mu}V^{0}(t,x_{0},\mu,y))
is continuous on
[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
;

2.

(t,x_{0},x,\mu)\mapsto(\partial_{t}V(t,x_{0},x,\mu),\nabla_{x_{0}}V(t,x_{0},x,\mu),\nabla_{x}V(t,x_{0},x,\mu),\nabla^{2}_{x_{0}}V(t,x_{0},x,\mu),\nabla_{x}^{2}V(t,x_{0},x,\mu))
is continuous on
[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
;

(t,x_{0},x,\mu,y)\mapsto(\partial_{\mu}V(t,x_{0},x,\mu,y),\nabla_{y}\partial_{\mu}V(t,x_{0},x,\mu,y))
is continuous on
[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
.

Assume also that

(t,x_{0},\mu)\mapsto\nabla_{x_{0}}V^{0}(t,x_{0},\mu)
and
(t,x_{0},x,\mu)\mapsto(\nabla_{x_{0}}V(t,x_{0},x,\mu),\nabla_{x}V(t,x_{0},x,\mu))
are Lipschitz
continuous with respect to
(x_{0},\mu)
and
(x_{0},x,\mu)
respectively
(using the distance
{\mathbb{W}}_{1}
to handle the argument
\mu
)
and that the initial condition

X^{0}_{0}
(in Subsection

2.1
)
is square-integrable.
Then,
the triplets
(X_{t}^{0},Y_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})_{0\leq t\leq T}
and

(\mu_{t},u_{t},v_{t}^{0})_{0\leq t\leq T}

obtained by solving,
on a product structure comprising
two filtered probability spaces

(\Omega^{0},{\mathcal{F}}^{0},{\mathbb{F}}^{0},{\mathbb{P}}^{0})

and

(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{F}},{{\mathbb{P}}})
equipped with two
Brownian motions
(B_{t}^{0})_{0\leq t\leq T}

and
(B_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
with values in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, the (coupled forward) equations

(2.29)

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{0}=-\nabla_{p}H^{0}\bigl(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}V^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})\bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{0}\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{0},\quad t\in[0,T],\\
&\partial_{t}\mu_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}\mu_{t}-\textrm{\rm div}_{x}\bigl(\nabla_{p}H(x,\nabla_{x}V(t,X_{t}^{0},x,\mu_{t}))\mu_{t}\bigr)=0,\quad(t,x)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d},\end{split}

and then by letting

(2.30)

\begin{split} &Y_{t}^{0}:=V^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t}),\quad Z_{t}^{0}:=\nabla_{x_{0}}V^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t}),\quad t\in[0,T],\\
&u_{t}(x):=V(t,X_{t}^{0},x,\mu_{t}),\quad v_{t}^{0}(x):=\nabla_{x_{0}}V(t,X_{t}^{0},x,\mu_{t}),\quad(t,x)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d},\end{split}

are solutions of
(
2.11
)–(
2.12
).

As the proof shows (see (
2.32
)), the solutions to (
2.11
)–(
2.12
) that we obtain in this manner just supply us with ‘true’ martingales in
the two equations.
Notice that, to simplify, we do not
prove that the
the triplets
(X_{t}^{0},Y_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})_{0\leq t\leq T}
and

(\mu_{t},u_{t},v_{t}^{0})_{0\leq t\leq T}

satisfy all the conditions in Definition

2.10
, as this would be useless at this stage of the paper. Obviously, this would require further assumptions on
V^{0}
and
V
.

Our main statement regarding the solvability of the master equation is

Theorem 2.16
.

In addition to Assumption
(
A
)
, assume that

i.

the coefficient
(t,x_{0},\mu)\mapsto f_{t}^{0}(x_{0},\mu)
is Hölder continuous in time, uniformly in

(x_{0},\mu)
; the coefficient
(t,x_{0},x,\mu)\mapsto f_{t}(x_{0},x,\mu)
is Hölder continuous in time,
uniformly in
(x_{0},x,\mu)
;

ii.

x_{0}\mapsto g^{0}(x_{0},\mu)
has
Hölder continuous second-order derivatives,
uniformly in
\mu
;
x_{0}\mapsto g(x_{0},x,\mu)

has Hölder continuous second-order derivatives,
uniformly in
(x,\mu)
.

Then, for any
T>0
and for
\sigma_{0}\geq\sigma_{0}^{*}(T)\in(0,+\infty)

(with the latter being defined as in the statement of Theorem
2.14
),
the system
(
2.27
)–(
2.28
)
admits a unique solution in the class of functions

(V^{0},V)

that satisfy items (1) and (2) in the statement of Proposition

2.15

and such that: (3)

(t,x_{0},\mu)\mapsto\nabla_{x_{0}}V^{0}(t,x_{0},\mu)
and
(t,x_{0},x,\mu)\mapsto(\nabla_{x_{0}}V(t,x_{0},x,\mu),\nabla_{x}V(t,x_{0},x,\mu))
are Lipschitz
continuous with respect to
(x_{0},\mu)
and
(x_{0},x,\mu)
respectively
(using the distance
{\mathbb{W}}_{1}
to handle the argument
\mu
);
(4)
(t,x_{0},\mu)\mapsto(V^{0}(t,x_{0},\mu),\nabla_{x_{0}}V^{0}(t,x_{0},\mu))
is globally bounded,

(t,x_{0},\mu)\mapsto(\|V(t,x_{0},\cdot,\mu)\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}},\|\nabla_{x_{0}}V(t,x_{0},\cdot,\mu)\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}})
is globally
bounded for any
\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\in[1,\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)]\setminus{\mathbb{N}}
.

If Assumption
(
B
)
is also in force, then existence and uniqueness
hold true on any interval
[0,T]
,
T>0
, and for any
\sigma_{0}\geq\sigma_{0}^{*}

(with the latter being defined as in the statement of Theorem
2.14
).

The proof of Theorem
2.16
is deferred to Section
4
, see Theorem

4.12
for a refined version.

We now turn to the proof of Proposition
2.15
:

To establish the solvability of (
2.29
), we consider the conditional McKean-Vlasov equation

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{0}=-\nabla_{p}H^{0}\Bigl(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}V^{0}\bigl(t,X_{t}^{0},{\mathcal{L}}^{0}(X_{t})\bigr)\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{0}\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{0},\\
&\mathrm{d}X_{t}=-\nabla_{p}H\Bigl(X_{t}^{0},X_{t},\nabla_{x}V\bigl(t,X_{t}^{0},X_{t},{\mathcal{L}}^{0}(X_{t})\bigr)\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}B_{t},\quad t\in[0,T],\end{split}

with the same initial conditions
X_{0}^{0}
and
X_{0}
as in Subsection
2.1
.

Because
\nabla_{x_{0}}V^{0}
and
\nabla_{x}V
are
jointly continuous in all the arguments
and
Lipschitz continuous
with respect to
(x_{0},\mu)
and
(x_{0},x,\mu)
respectively
(Lipschitz continuity in the argument
\mu
holding true with respect to
{\mathbb{W}}_{1}
), the above system has
a unique solution, see
[
20
, Proposition 2.8]
.
It satisfies

(2.31)

{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\Bigl[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}|X_{t}^{0}|^{2}\Bigr]<\infty.

(A similar bound holds true for
\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}|X_{t}|^{2}
under

{\mathbb{E}}^{0}{\mathbb{E}}
when
{\bm{X}}
is implicitly regarded as being

{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
-valued, but this bound has little interest since

{\bm{X}}
is regarded as being

{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
-valued.)
Letting
(\mu_{t}:={\mathcal{L}}^{0}(X_{t}))_{0\leq t\leq T}
,
this makes it possible to define
(Y_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})_{0\leq t\leq T}
and

(u_{t},v_{t}^{0})_{0\leq t\leq T}
as in
(
2.30
). Combining the integrability condition
(
2.31
)
with
the regularity properties of
\nabla_{x_{0}}V^{0}
,
\nabla_{x_{0}}V
and
\nabla_{x}V
, we get

(2.32)

{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\Bigl[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\bigl(|Z_{t}^{0}|^{2}+\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}|\nabla_{x}V(t,X_{t}^{0},x,\mu_{t})|^{2}+\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}|v_{t}^{0}(x)|^{2}\bigr)\Bigr]<\infty,

The derivation of the forward equation in
(
2.12
) is straightforward.
As for the two backward equations
in
(
2.11
)
and
(
2.12
), they are obtained by combining the chain rule proved in Appendix, see Proposition
6.1
, with
the two PDEs
(
2.27
) and (
2.28
).
The bound
(
2.32
)
shows that the martingale terms are ‘true’ martingales.
∎

2.7
About Fokker-Planck equations with random coefficients

The purpose of this subsection is to prove
Lemmas

2.4

and

2.9
.

We call
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
the (completion of the) probability measure on
\Omega_{\textrm{\rm canon}}
under which the canonical
process
({\bm{X}},{\bm{B}})
satisfies:
(i)
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\circ X_{0}^{-1}=\mu
;
(ii)
{\bm{B}}
is an
{\mathbb{F}}
-Brownian motion starting from
0
;
(iii)
X_{t}-X_{0}=B_{t}
for all
t\in[0,T]
.

For
\omega\in\Omega^{0}
,
we then let

\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\omega^{0}}:={\mathscr{E}}_{T}\biggl(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\alpha\bigl(t,X_{t}^{0}(\omega^{0}),X_{t},\mu_{t}(\omega^{0})\bigr)\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{t}\biggr)\cdot\tilde{\mathbb{P}}.

Since
\alpha

is bounded,

\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\omega^{0}}
is a probability measure.
It is standard to check that, under

\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\omega^{0}}
, the process

(\tilde{B}_{t}:=B_{t}-\int_{0}^{t}\alpha(s,X_{s}^{0}(\omega^{0}),X_{s},\mu_{s}(\omega^{0}))\mathrm{d}s)_{0\leq t\leq T}
is an
{\mathbb{F}}
-Brownian motion starting from
0
,
X_{0}

is distributed according to
\mu
and, for any
t\in[0,T]
,

(2.33)

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\alpha\bigl(t,X_{t}^{0}(\omega^{0}),X_{t},\mu_{t}(\omega^{0})\bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}\tilde{B}_{t},\quad t\in[0,T].

We then define

{\mathbb{P}}_{\omega^{0}}:=\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\omega^{0}}\circ\bigl({\bm{X}},\tilde{\bm{B}}\bigr)^{-1}.

Using Fubini’s theorem,
it is easy to prove that, for any event
C
of
{\mathcal{B}}(\Omega_{\textrm{\rm canon}})
,
the mapping
\omega^{0}\mapsto{\mathbb{P}}_{\omega^{0}}(C)
is measurable, which proves the measurability
of the mapping

\Omega_{\textrm{\rm canon}}^{0}\ni\omega^{0}\mapsto{\mathbb{P}}_{\omega^{0}}
.

Using the fact that the SDE
(
2.7
)
(with a prescribed initial condition)
is uniquely strongly (and thus weakly) solvable for any
\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}_{\textrm{\rm canon}}
(because
\alpha
is bounded), we get that

{\mathbb{P}}_{\omega^{0}}
is unique.
∎

First Step.

With
{\bm{x}}^{0}:=(x_{t}^{0})_{0\leq t\leq T}
an element of
{\mathcal{C}}([0,T],{\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, we
associate the Fokker-Planck equation

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\tilde{\mu}_{t}^{{\bm{x}}^{0}}=\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}\tilde{\mu}_{t}^{{\bm{x}}^{0}}-\textrm{\rm div}_{x}\Bigl(\alpha\bigl(t,x_{t}^{0},\cdot,\tilde{\mu}_{t}^{{\bm{x}}^{0}}\bigr)\tilde{\mu}_{t}^{{\bm{x}}^{0}}\Bigr),\quad t\in[0,T],\end{split}

with
\mu_{0}
as initial condition (at time
0
).

By

[
42
]
and
Remark

2.5
,
we know that, for any
{\bm{x}}^{0}\in{\mathcal{C}}([0,T],{\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, the Fokker-Planck equation right above admits a unique
solution in the
same weak sense as in
Remark

2.3
, which can be obtained by iterating the mapping, denoted
\Phi({\bm{x}}^{0},\cdot)
, that sends an element

{\bm{\nu}}=(\nu_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}\in{\mathcal{C}}([0,T],{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}))
onto the solution

{\bm{\mu}}=(\mu_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}

of the
equation

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\mu_{t}=\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}\mu_{t}-\textrm{\rm div}_{x}\bigl(\alpha\bigl(t,x_{t}^{0},\cdot,\nu_{t}\bigr)\mu_{t}\bigr),\quad t\in[0,T],\end{split}

with
\mu_{0}
as initial condition.
By
Lemma

2.4
,
(\mu_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is the flow of marginal laws of the
process
{\bm{X}}
under
the tilted measure

{\mathbb{P}}_{{\bm{x}}^{0}}:={\mathscr{E}}_{T}\biggl(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\alpha(t,x_{t}^{0},X_{0}+B_{t},\nu_{t})\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{t}\biggr)\cdot\tilde{\mathbb{P}},

where

\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
is the same probability measure on
\Omega_{\textrm{\rm canon}}

as in the proof of Lemma
2.4
. Proceeding as in the latter proof,
we easily deduce that

{\bm{\mu}}
, seen as an element of
{\mathcal{C}}([0,T],{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}))
, is
the image by a
measurable function
\Phi
of
the pair
({\bm{x}}^{0},{\bm{\nu}})
, seen as an element of

{\mathcal{C}}([0,T],{\mathbb{R}}^{d})\times{\mathcal{C}}([0,T],{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}))
, i.e.,

{\bm{\mu}}=\Phi({\bm{x}}^{0},{\bm{\nu}})
.
And then, writing

{\bm{\mu}}^{{\bm{x}}^{0}}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\bigl[\Phi({\bm{x}}^{0},\cdot)\bigr]^{\circ n}({\bm{\nu}}^{0}),

for an arbitrarily fixed element

{\bm{\nu}}^{0}\in{\mathcal{C}}([0,T],{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}))
,
we deduce that the mapping

{\mathcal{C}}([0,T],{\mathbb{R}}^{d})\ni{\bm{x}}^{0}\mapsto{\bm{\mu}}^{{\bm{x}}^{0}}\in{\mathcal{C}}([0,T],{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}))
is measurable.

Considering on an arbitrary probability space a
d
-dimensional Brownian
motion
(\bar{B}_{t}^{0})_{0\leq t\leq T}
and replacing
{\bm{x}}^{0}
by

(x_{0}+\bar{B}_{t}^{0})_{0\leq t\leq T}
, we get the existence of a (measurable) solution to

\begin{split} &\mathrm{d}\bar{X}_{t}^{0}=\mathrm{d}\bar{B}_{t}^{0},\\
&\partial_{t}\bar{\mu}_{t}=\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}\bar{\mu}_{t}-\textrm{\rm div}_{x}\bigl(\alpha(t,\bar{X}_{t}^{0},\cdot,\bar{\mu}_{t})\bar{\mu}_{t}\bigr),\quad t\in[0,T],\end{split}

with
(x_{0},\mu)
as initial condition (at time
0
). In fact, the solution is (replacing the interval
[0,T]
by the interval
[0,S]
, for
S
running between
0
and

T
in the above measurability argument) progressively-measurable with respect to the filtration generated by

\bar{\bm{B}}^{0}
. Also, it is pathwise unique.
Below, we call

\bar{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
the law of
(\bar{\bm{X}}^{0},\bar{\bm{\mu}},\bar{\bm{B}}^{0})

on
\Omega^{0}_{\textrm{\rm canon}}
.
This proves (b) in the statement.

Second Step.

Assume now that the BMO condition
(
2.10
)
is satisfied
under

\bar{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
.
Under
the latter probability,
the canonical process
({\bm{X}}^{0},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{B}}^{0})

on
\Omega^{0}_{\textrm{\rm canon}}
satisfies

\begin{split} &\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{0}=\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{0},\\
&\partial_{t}\mu_{t}=\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}\mu_{t}-\textrm{\rm div}_{x}\bigl(\alpha(t,X_{t}^{0},\cdot,\mu_{t})\mu_{t}\bigr),\quad t\in[0,T],\end{split}

with
(x_{0},\mu)
as initial condition (at time
0
). We introduce the tilted measure

\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{0}:={\mathscr{E}}_{T}\biggl(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\alpha^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{0}\biggr)\cdot\bar{\mathbb{P}}^{0},

which is a probability measure thanks to the BMO condition (
2.5
).
Letting
(\tilde{B}^{0}_{t}:=B_{t}^{0}-\int_{0}^{t}\alpha^{0}(s,X_{s}^{0},\mu_{s})\mathrm{d}s)_{0\leq t\leq T}
, we have

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{0}=\alpha^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}\tilde{B}_{t}^{0},\\
&\partial_{t}\mu_{t}=\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x_{0}}\mu_{t}-\textrm{\rm div}_{x_{0}}\bigl(\alpha(t,X_{t}^{0},\cdot,\mu_{t})\mu_{t}\bigr),\quad t\in[0,T],\end{split}

with
(x_{0},\mu)
as initial condition (at time
0
),
and
\tilde{\bm{B}}^{0}
is a Brownian motion under
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
.
It then remains to let

{\mathbb{P}}^{0}:=\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{0}\circ({\bm{X}}^{0},{\bm{\mu}},\tilde{\bm{B}}^{0})^{-1}
.
It is a probability measure on

\Omega^{0}_{\textrm{\rm canon}}

and it
satisfies the requirements of
Lemma
2.9
.
Notice in particular that item (iv) in Definition

2.2

follows from

[
40
, Theorems 2.3 and 3.3]
.
This proves the existence part in item (c) in the statement.

Third Step.

Uniqueness is proven in a similar manner. Assuming that we are given a probability measure
(still denoted)
{\mathbb{P}}^{0}

satisfying items (i), (ii), (iii) and (iv) in
Definition

2.2
and considering without any loss of generality its completion, we introduce the tilted measure

\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{0}:={\mathscr{E}}_{T}\biggl(-\int_{0}^{\cdot}\alpha^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{0}\biggr)\cdot{\mathbb{P}}^{0},

which a probability measure thanks to the BMO condition
(
2.5
)
under
{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
.

Letting
(\tilde{B}^{0}_{t}:=X_{t}^{0}-x_{0})_{0\leq t\leq T}
, we have

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{0}=\mathrm{d}\tilde{B}_{t}^{0},\\
&\partial_{t}\mu_{t}=\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x_{0}}\mu_{t}-\textrm{\rm div}_{x_{0}}\bigl(\alpha(t,X_{t}^{0},\cdot,\mu_{t})\mu_{t}\bigr),\quad t\in[0,T],\end{split}

and
\tilde{\bm{B}}^{0}
is a Brownian motion under
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
.
And by the uniqueness result established in the first step, we see that the law of

({\bm{X}}^{0},{\bm{\mu}},\tilde{\bm{B}}^{0})
under
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
is equal to the probability

\bar{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
constructed in the second step.
By
[
40
, Theorems 2.3 and 3.3]
again,
the BMO condition (
2.10
) is
satisfied under
\bar{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
.
Also, we have

{\mathbb{P}}^{0}={\mathscr{E}}_{T}\biggl(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\alpha^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})\cdot\mathrm{d}\tilde{B}_{t}^{0}\biggr)\cdot\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{0}.

In the end,

{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
, which is tautologically equal to the law of

({\bm{X}}^{0},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{B}}^{0})

under
{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
,
can be regarded as the law of

({\bm{X}}^{0},{\bm{\mu}},(\tilde{B}_{t}^{0}-\int_{0}^{\cdot}\alpha^{0}(s,X_{s}^{0},\mu_{s})\mathrm{d}s)_{0\leq t\leq T})

under
{\mathscr{E}}_{T}(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\alpha^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})\cdot\mathrm{d}\tilde{B}_{t}^{0})\cdot\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
.
Since

\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{0}\circ({\bm{X}}^{0},{\bm{\mu}},\tilde{\bm{B}}^{0})^{-1}=\bar{\mathbb{P}}^{0}\circ({\bm{X}}^{0},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{B}}^{0})^{-1}
,
we deduce that

{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
coincides with the law of

({\bm{X}}^{0},{\bm{\mu}},(B_{t}^{0}-\int_{0}^{\cdot}\alpha^{0}(s,X_{s}^{0},\mu_{s})\mathrm{d}s)_{0\leq t\leq T})

under
{\mathscr{E}}_{T}(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\alpha^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{0})\cdot\bar{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
.
This is exactly the construction achieved in the previous step.
∎

3
A priori estimates for the forward-backward system

The objective of this section is to obtain a series of
a priori
estimates for the solution(s)
to the forward-backward system
(
2.11
)–(
2.12
), when
posed on an arbitrary
filtered probability space

(\Omega^{0},{\mathcal{F}}^{0},{\mathbb{F}}^{0},{\mathbb{P}}^{0})

satisfying the usual conditions and
equipped with a
Brownian motion
(B_{t}^{0})_{0\leq t\leq T}
with values in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,
with
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
being
generated by
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}^{0}

and
{\bm{B}}^{0}
.

3.1
HJB equation for the minor player

The main result of this subsection concerns the regularity of the solution to the
stochastic HJB equation in
(
2.12
). We proceed very much as in the monograph
[
14
]
.
We also refer to

[
29
]
for a related analysis but in Sobolev (instead of Hölder) spaces.

Throughout this subsection, we fix an

{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-adapted
continuous path
{\bm{X}}^{0}=(X_{t}^{0})_{0\leq t\leq T}
with values in

{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
(not necessarily solving the forward equation in
(
2.11
)) and an
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-adapted continuous path

{\bm{\mu}}=(\mu_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
with values in
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})

(not necessarily solving the forward equation in
(
2.12
)). With the two of them,
we associate the (backward) stochastic Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman equation

(3.1)

\begin{split} &\mathrm{d}_{t}u_{t}(x)=\Bigl(-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}u_{t}(x)+H\bigl(x,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x)\bigr)-f_{t}(X_{t}^{0},x,\mu_{t})\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}m_{t}(x),\quad(t,x)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d},\\
&u_{T}(x)=g(X_{T}^{0},x,\mu_{T}),\quad x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d},\end{split}

where
(m_{t}(x))_{0\leq t\leq T}
is an
\mathbb{F}^{0}
-martingale for any
x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
.

The class within which the equation (
3.1
) is solved is clarified in the following statement, which is taken
from
[
14
, Proposition 4.3.8]
:

Lemma 3.1
.

Under Assumption
(
A
)
and within the framework described above,
the equation (
3.1
)
has a unique solution
({\bm{u}},{\bm{m}})=(u_{t},m_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
, such that

1.

(u_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is an

{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-adapted process with
values in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\text{{s}}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
, with continuous
paths in

{\mathcal{C}}^{\text{{r}}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
for any

\text{{r}}<\text{{s}}
,
satisfying

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|u_{t}\|_{\text{{s}}}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega^{0},{\mathcal{F}}^{0},{\mathbb{P}}^{0})
.

2.

(m_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is an

{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-adapted process with
values in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\text{{s}}-2}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
,
with continuous paths in

{\mathcal{C}}^{\text{{r}}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
for any

\text{{r}}<\text{{s}}-2
,
satisfying

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|m_{t}\|_{\text{{s}}-2}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega^{0},{\mathcal{F}}^{0},{\mathbb{P}}^{0})
, with
m_{0} \equiv 0
, and
with
(m_{t}(x))_{0\leq t\leq T}
being an
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-martingale
for any
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
.

In fact, uniqueness holds in a wider class of solutions
({\bm{u}},{\bm{m}})
for which the above holds true
with respect to some
\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}^{\prime}>2
in lieu of
s
.

The
result mainly follows from

[
14
, Proposition 4.3.8]
,
with two main differences: (i) one must here handle a quadratic HJB equation, whilst the nonlinearity
is of linear growth in
[
14
]
; (ii) one
here claims that continuity of
{\bm{u}}
holds in

{\mathcal{C}}^{\text{{r}}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
for any

\text{{r}}<\text{{s}}
, whilst continuity
in

[
14
]

is obtained up to the order
\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor
; (iii)
as explained in
the third item of
Remark

2.11
, the pair
({\bm{u}},{\bm{m}})

is directly seen as
a random variable with values in
non-integer Hölder spaces, which are not separable. Generally speaking, the argument to treat (ii)
is as follows: using the fact that solutions are proven to satisfy

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|u_{t}\|_{\text{{s}}}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega^{0},{\mathcal{F}}^{0},{\mathbb{P}}^{0})
,
continuity with values in any

{\mathcal{C}}^{\text{{r}}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
, with

\text{{r}}<\text{{s}}
,
follows from the fact that the embedding from

{\mathcal{C}}^{\text{{r}}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})

to

{\mathcal{C}}^{\text{{s}}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})

is compact.
As for (iii), we follow the outline
given in
Remark

2.11
. Once

{\bm{u}}
is known to be an
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-adapted
(continuous) process with values in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-1}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
,
the same argument based on Schauder’s estimates as the one
used in
[
14
]
permits to show that, for any
t\in[0,T)
,

u_{t}
takes values in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}^{\prime}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
and

m_{t}
in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}^{\prime}-2}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
, for a certain
{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}^{\prime}>{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}
.
Then, using the same argument as in
the proof of
[
49
, Proposition II.2, (1b)]
, we deduce that, for any

t\in[0,T)
,
u_{t}
and
m_{t}
are
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{0}
-
Bochner
measurable
with values in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
and

{\mathcal{C}}^{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-2}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
respectively.
At time
t=T
, the field
x\mapsto g(X_{T}^{0},x,\mu_{T})
can be proven to
be
Bochner
measurable:
X_{T}^{0}
and
\mu_{T}
are the almost sure limits of
simple random variables with values in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and

{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
respectively. Using the fact that the function

(x_{0},\mu)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})\mapsto g(x_{0},\cdot,\mu)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})

is continuous, we deduce that

u_{T}
is the almost sure limit of
simple random variables with values in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
, which
shows
Bochner
measurability.
It remains to prove that

m_{T}
is also

Bochner
measurable with values in

{\mathcal{C}}^{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-2}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
. In fact, it suffices to prove that
the integral from
0
to
T
of the driver in the backward equation of
(
3.1
)
is
Bochner
measurable with values in

{\mathcal{C}}^{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-2}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
.
By

[
49
, Proposition II.2, (2)]
, it suffices to prove
the same property but for the integral from
0
to
T-\varepsilon
, and this for any

\varepsilon\in(0,T)
. The latter is a mere consequence of the fact that
the integral from
0
to
T-\varepsilon
takes in fact values in

{\mathcal{C}}^{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}^{\prime}-2}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
for a certain

\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}^{\prime}>\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}
.

In order to tackle (i), we proceed by considering first a truncated version of the Hamiltonian, namely we consider a function
H^{R}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
such that

\begin{split}&H^{R}(x,p)=H(x,p),\quad\textrm{\rm if}\ |p|\leq R\ ;\quad\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\sup_{p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|\nabla_{p}H^{R}(x,p)|<\infty,\\
&\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\sup_{p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|\nabla_{x}H^{R}(x,p)|\leq\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\sup_{p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|\nabla_{x}H(x,p)|+1.\end{split}

Then,
[
14
, Proposition 4.3.8]
applies to the following equation:

(3.2)

\begin{split} &\mathrm{d}_{t}u_{t}^{R}(x)=\Bigl(-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}u_{t}^{R}(x)+H^{R}\bigl(x,\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{R}(x)\bigr)-f_{t}(X_{t}^{0},x,\mu_{t})\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}m_{t}^{R}(x),\quad(t,x)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d},\\
&u_{T}^{R}(x)=g(X_{T}^{0},x,\mu_{T}),\quad x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d},\end{split}

and supplies us with the existence of a unique solution
({\bm{u}}^{R},{\bm{m}}^{R})
satisfying the prescriptions of
items 1 and 2 in the statement of Lemma
3.1
.

In order to pass from the equation (
3.2
) to the original equation (
3.1
), it suffices to show that

|\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{R}|
can be bounded by a deterministic constant independent of
R
, which can be done by the classical Bernstein
argument. Differentiating with respect to
x
(which is licit from the results in
[
14
, Proposition 4.3.8]
), we have

(3.3)

\begin{split} &\mathrm{d}_{t}\partial_{x_{i}}u_{t}^{R}(x)=\Bigl(-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}\partial_{x_{i}}u_{t}^{R}(x)+\nabla_{p}H^{R}\bigl(x,\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{R}(x)\bigr)\cdot\partial_{x_{i}}\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{R}(x)+\partial_{x_{i}}H^{R}\bigl(x,\nabla_{x}u_{t}^{R}(x)\bigr)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-\partial_{x_{i}}f_{t}(X_{t}^{0},x,\mu_{t})\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}\partial_{x_{i}}m_{t}^{R}(x),\quad(t,x)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d},\\
&\partial_{x_{i}}u_{T}^{R}(x)=\partial_{x_{i}}g(X_{T}^{0},x,\mu_{T}),\quad x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d},\end{split}

for any coordinate
i\in\{1,\cdots,d\}
. Then, we can interpret the equation as a backward stochastic transport diffusion equation, for which we have a maximum
principle. In turn, we get that

|\partial_{x_{i}}u_{t}^{R}(x)|\leq\|\nabla_{x}g\|_{L^{\infty}}+T\|\nabla_{x}f\|_{L^{\infty}}+T\bigl(\|\nabla_{x}H\|_{L^{\infty}}+1\bigr).

We hence get a bound
C
for the left-hand side. Obviously, this bound
C
is deterministic and independent of
R
, which allows us to retrieve the original equation by choosing
R
larger than
C
. Uniqueness of the hence constructed solution
is obvious because we restricted our analysis to solutions that are bounded in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\text{{s}}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
by a deterministic constant.
As mentioned in the statement, we can even state uniqueness in a wider class. This follows from the fact that the parameter

n
in

[
14
, Proposition 4.3.8]

can be taken equal to 2.
This completes the proof.
∎

Since
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
is
generated by
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}^{0}

and
{\bm{B}}^{0}
, we can represent, for any

x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
, the martingale
(m_{t}(x))_{0\leq t\leq T}
in the form

(3.4)

m_{t}(x)=\sigma_{0}\int_{0}^{t}v_{s}^{0}(x)\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{s}^{0},\quad 0\leq t\leq T,

where
(v_{t}^{0}(x))_{0\leq t\leq T}

is an
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
-valued
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-progressively measurable process.
The regularity of
v^{0}
is given by the following statement:

Lemma 3.2
.

The representation term in (
3.4
)
induces a process

(v_{t}^{0})_{0\leq t\leq T}

with values in
{\mathcal{H}}^{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})

(which is embedded in

{\mathcal{C}}^{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-d/2-1}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
). Moreover,

(3.5)

{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\int_{0}^{T}\|v_{t}^{0}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-d/2-1}^{2}\mathrm{d}s<\infty,

and, for any
k\in\{0,\cdots,\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-d/2\rfloor-1\}
,

{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
-almost surely, for all
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
,

(3.6)

\nabla^{k}_{x}m_{t}(x)=\sigma_{0}\int_{0}^{t}\nabla^{k}_{x}v_{s}^{0}(x)\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{s}^{0},\quad t\in[0,T].

Notice that

{\mathcal{H}}^{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})

(equipped with
\|\cdot\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-d/2-1}
) is separable:
for any
t\in[0,T]
,

v_{t}^{0}
, when regarded as a random variable with values in

{\mathcal{C}}^{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-d/2-1}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
is obviously

Bochner
measurable.

Because

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|u_{t}\|_{\text{{s}}}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega^{0},{\mathcal{F}}^{0},{\mathbb{P}}^{0})
,
we can see the equation
(
3.1
)
as a linear equation with a source
term in

{\mathcal{C}}^{\text{{s}}-1}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
.
This makes it possible
to apply
[
30
, Theorem 2.3]
(with a modicum of care because the latter result is stated on the Euclidean space,
but the adaptation to the periodic setting is straightforward).
We deduce that
the representation term

(v_{t}^{0})_{0\leq t\leq T}
in
(
3.4
)
takes values in

{\mathcal{H}}^{\lfloor\text{{s}}\rfloor}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
. Moreover, it satisfies

{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\int_{0}^{T}\|v_{t}^{0}\|_{\lfloor\text{{s}}\rfloor,2}^{2}\mathrm{d}s<\infty.

By Sobolev embedding, we obtain
(
3.5
) (because

\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-d/2>\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-d/2-1
).
Then,
(
3.6
)
can be easily established by induction on
k\in\{1,\cdots,\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-d/2\rfloor-1\}
, by passing to the limit
(as
\varepsilon
tends to
0
)
in the
representation
formula

\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon} \Bigl(\nabla_{x}^{k-1}m_{t}(x+\varepsilon\xi)-\nabla_{x}^{k-1}m_{t}(x)\Bigr)=\sigma_{0}\int_{0}^{t}\Bigl[\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon} \Bigl(\nabla_{x}^{k-1}v^{0}_{s}(x+\varepsilon\xi)-\nabla_{x}^{k-1}v^{0}_{s}(x)\Bigr)\Bigr]\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{s},

for any
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
and unitary
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. This proves
that, for all
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
,

{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
-almost surely,
for all
t\in[0,T]
,
(
3.6
) holds true.
Using the regularity properties
of the left-hand side
in
(
3.6
), we
easily deduce that, for any integer

p\geq 1
, there exists
a constant
C_{p}
such that, for any

k\in\{0,\cdots,\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-d/2\rfloor-1\}

and any
x,x^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
,

{\mathbb{E}}\biggl[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\biggl|\int_{0}^{t}\Bigl(\nabla^{k}_{x}v_{s}^{0}(x^{\prime})-\nabla^{k}_{x}v_{s}^{0}(x)\Bigr)\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{s}^{0}\biggr|^{p}\biggr]\ ={\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}|\nabla^{k}m_{t}(x^{\prime})-\nabla^{k}m_{t}(x)|^{p}\Bigr]\leq C_{p}|x^{\prime}-x|^{p},

which proves, by Kolmogorov continuity theorem, that we can find, for each
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
, a version of the stochastic process

(\int_{0}^{t}\nabla_{x}^{k}v^{0}_{s}(x)\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{s})_{0\leq t\leq T}

that is continuous in
x
. This permits to exchange the quantifiers
in
(
3.6
)
and then get the formula
{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
-almost surely,
for all
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
.
∎

3.2
Uniform in time estimates for the HJB equation

Within the same framework as in Subsection

3.1
, we now provide a uniform in time estimate of the gradient of
the solution to
(
3.1
). This bound is key in our analysis.

Proposition 3.3
.

Under Assumption
(
A
)
, there exists a constant
R_{0}
, only depending on the parameters in
(
A
)
except
(\sigma_{0},T)
(in particular,
R_{0}
is independent of
{\bm{\mu}}
), such that

(3.7)

{\mathbb{P}}^{0}\Bigl(\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|\nabla_{x}u_{t}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq R_{0}\Bigr)=1.

The proof of the bound (
3.7
) is inspired from the proof of Lemma 1.5 in
[
16
]
, but adapted to the SPDE setting.

The very first step of the proof is to consider, for a given
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
with
|\xi|=1
,
the equation satisfied by
(D^{2}_{\xi\xi}u_{t}(x):=(\nabla^{2}_{xx}u_{t}(x)\xi)\cdot\xi)_{0\leq t\leq T,x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}.

By a straightforward adaptation of the computations as in
[
16
]
, we get that

D^{2}_{\xi\xi}u
solves the following equation:

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}_{t}\Bigl(D^{2}_{\xi\xi}u_{t}(x)\Bigr)&=\Bigl[-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}D^{2}_{\xi\xi}u_{t}(x)+\Bigl(\nabla^{2}_{xx}H\bigl(x,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x)\bigr)\xi\Bigr)\cdot\xi+2\Bigl(\nabla_{px}^{2}H\bigl(x,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x)\bigr)\xi\Bigr)\cdot\nabla_{x}D_{\xi}u_{t}(x)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\bigl(\nabla^{2}_{pp}H\bigl(x,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x)\bigr)\nabla_{x}D_{\xi}u_{t}(x)\bigr)\cdot\nabla_{x}D_{\xi}u_{t}(x)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\nabla_{p}H\bigl(x,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x)\bigr)\cdot\nabla_{x}D^{2}_{\xi\xi}u_{t}(x)-D^{2}_{\xi\xi}f_{t}(X_{t}^{0},x,\mu_{t})\Bigr]\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\mathrm{d}\bigl(D^{2}_{\xi\xi}m_{t}\bigr)(x),\end{split}

where the notations
D_{\xi}
and
D^{2}_{\xi\xi}
are extended in an obvious manner to the first and second order derivatives
in the direction
\xi
of (possibly random) functions different from
u_{t}(\cdot)
.
Above,
(D^{2}_{\xi\xi}m_{t}(x))_{0\leq t\leq T}
is a martingale.

The key point in the proof is to observe that
there exists a constant
C>0
, only depending on the parameters in (
A5
), such that, for
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
,
p,q\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,

(3.8)

\begin{split}&\Bigl(\nabla^{2}_{xx}H(x,p)\xi\Bigr)\cdot\xi+2\Bigl(\nabla_{px}^{2}H(x,p)\xi\Bigr)\cdot q+\Bigl(\nabla^{2}_{pp}H(x,p)q\Bigr)\cdot q-D^{2}_{\xi\xi}f_{t}(X_{t}^{0},x,\mu_{t})\\
&\geq C^{-1}|q|^{2}-C.\end{split}

We next consider the same Brownian motion
(B_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
as in the dynamics of the minor player. In particular,

(B_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is independent of the common noise
(B_{t}^{0})_{0\leq t\leq T}
. We then consider the SDE

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=-\nabla_{p}H\bigl(X_{t},\nabla_{x}u_{t}(X_{t})\bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}B_{t},\quad t\in[t_{0},T],

for a given
t_{0}\in[0,T]
and for an initial condition
X_{t_{0}}=x
.
Notice that the drift is random, but the solution is uniquely defined thanks to the regularity properties
granted by Lemma

3.1
.
We also let

U_{t}:=D^{2}_{\xi\xi}u_{t}(X_{t}).

By applying Itô-Wentzell formula
to
(U_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}

and by invoking
Lemma

3.2
to guarantee that

{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\int_{[0,T]}\|D^{2}_{\xi\xi}v^{0}_{t}\|^{2}_{1}\mathrm{d}t<\infty

(thanks to the condition
\text{{s}}>d/2+5
), we get (see footnote

1
for the proof)

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}U_{t}&=\Bigl[\Bigl(\nabla^{2}_{xx}H\bigl(X_{t},\nabla_{x}u_{t}(X_{t})\bigr)\xi\Bigr)\cdot\xi+2\Bigl(\nabla_{px}^{2}H\bigl(X_{t},\nabla_{x}u_{t}(X_{t})\bigr)\xi\Bigr)\cdot\nabla_{x}D_{\xi}u_{t}(X_{t})\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\bigl(\nabla^{2}_{pp}H\bigl(X_{t},\nabla_{x}u_{t}(X_{t})\bigr)\nabla_{x}D_{\xi}u_{t}(X_{t})\bigr)\cdot\nabla_{x}D_{\xi}u_{t}(X_{t})-D^{2}_{\xi\xi}f_{t}(X_{t}^{0},X_{t},\mu_{t})\Bigr]\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}n_{t},\end{split}

where
(n_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is a new generic martingale term, but now independent of the entry
x
.
By
(
3.8
) and thanks to the lower bound

|\nabla_{x}D_{\xi}u_{t}(X_{t})|^{2}\geq|\nabla_{x}D_{\xi}u_{t}(X_{t})\cdot\xi|^{2}=|D^{2}_{\xi\xi}u_{t}(X_{t})|^{2}=|U_{t}|^{2}
, we get

(3.9)

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}U_{t}&\geq\bigl(C^{-1}|U_{t}|^{2}-C\bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}n_{t}.\\
&=C^{-1}\bigl(U_{t}-C)\bigl(U_{t}+C\bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}n_{t},\end{split}

which gives

\mathrm{d}\Bigl[\exp\biggl(-C^{-1}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}(U_{s}+C)\mathrm{d}s\biggr)\bigl(U_{t}-C\bigr)\Bigr]\geq\mathrm{d}n_{t}.

In particular,
assuming without any loss of generality that
\|D^{2}_{\xi\xi}g\|_{\infty}\leq C
(i.e.
C\geq\kappa
,
with the notations used in Assumption (
A
)),
the boundary condition at time
T
in the above left-hand side has a non-positive value, from which we deduce (by conditioning on

{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}}^{0}\otimes{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}}
) that
U_{t_{0}}\leq C
.
By initializing
X
from any
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
at time
t_{0}
,
this shows that,
{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
-almost surely,

\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\sup_{\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}:|\xi|=1}\nabla^{2}_{x}u_{t_{0}}(x)\xi\cdot\xi\leq C.

By (15) in
[
16
]
, which says that, on the torus, the Lipschitz constant of a smooth function can be controlled by its
semi-concavity constant,
we deduce that
{\mathbb{P}}^{0}(\{\|\nabla_{x}u_{t_{0}}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq R_{0}\})=1
, with
R_{0}:=C\sqrt{d}
.
By continuity in time of the process
(\|u_{t}\|_{2})_{0\leq t\leq T}
, we complete the proof.
∎

We eventually extend the result of
Proposition

3.3

to higher-derivatives:

Proposition 3.4
.

Under Assumption
(
A
)

and within the context described in the beginning of Subsection

3.1
, there exists a constant
R_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-1}
, only depending on the parameters in
(
A
)
except
(\sigma_{0},T)
(in particular,
R
is independent of
{\bm{\mu}}
), such that

(3.10)

{\mathbb{P}}^{0}\Bigl(\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|\nabla_{x}u_{t}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-1}\leq R_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-1}\Bigr)=1.

When
t
is close to
T
(say
T-t\leq 1
), the result follows from
[
14
, Proposition 4.3.8]

(which applies since the gradient in the Hamiltonian is known to be bounded by means of
Proposition
3.3
).

In particular, we can assume that
T-t\geq 1
. The only difficulty is to get a bound that is independent of
T
. By
taking one derivative in the equation
(
3.1
)
as done in (
3.3
)
and then by using standard heat kernel estimates, we claim that, for any
integer
k\in\{1,\cdots,\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-1\}
, any
t,S\in[0,T]
, with
S-1\leq t<S
,

\begin{split}\|\nabla_{x}u_{t}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-k}&\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{S-t}}{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\bigl[\|\nabla_{x}u_{S}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-(k+1)}\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{0}\bigr]\\
&\hskip 5.0pt+C{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{t}^{S}\frac{1}{\sqrt{r-t}}\sup_{i=1,\cdots,d}\bigl\|\nabla_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{r}(\cdot)\bigr)\cdot\partial_{x_{i}}\nabla_{x}u_{r}\bigr\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-(k+1)}\mathrm{d}r\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{0}\biggr]\\
&\hskip 5.0pt+C{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{t}^{S}\frac{1}{\sqrt{r-t}}\Bigl(\bigl\|\nabla_{x}H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{r}(\cdot)\bigr)\bigr\|_{s-(k+1)}+\bigl\|\nabla_{x}f_{r}(X_{r}^{0},\cdot,\mu_{r})\bigr\|_{s-(k+1)}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}r\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{0}\biggr].\end{split}

Assuming that we have uniform (in time and in
\omega\in\Omega^{0}
) bounds for

\|\nabla_{x}u_{t}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-(k+1)}
, we deduce that there exists a constant
C_{k}
such that

(3.11)

\begin{split}\|\nabla_{x}u_{t}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-k}&\leq\frac{C_{k}}{\sqrt{S-t}} +C_{k}{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{t}^{S}\frac{1}{\sqrt{r-t}}\|\nabla_{x}u_{r}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-k}\mathrm{d}r\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{0}\biggr].\end{split}

And then, for any
\delta\in(0,S)
,
with
\delta\leq 1
,
(the mapping
t\mapsto\textrm{\rm essup}_{\omega\in\Omega^{0}}\|\nabla_{x}u_{t}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-k}

is measurable as limit of

t\mapsto{\mathbb{E}}^{0}[\|\nabla_{x}u_{t}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-k}^{p}]^{1/p}

as
p
tends to
\infty
)

\begin{split}&\int_{S-\delta}^{S}\frac{1}{\sqrt{t-(S-\delta)}}\textrm{\rm essup}_{\omega\in\Omega^{0}}\|\nabla_{x}u_{t}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-k}\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq C_{k}B(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}) +C_{k}\int_{S-\delta}^{S}\frac{1}{\sqrt{t-(S-\delta)}}\biggl(\int_{t}^{S}\frac{1}{\sqrt{r-t}}\textrm{\rm essup}_{\omega\in\Omega^{0}}\|\nabla_{x}u_{r}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-k}\mathrm{d}r\biggr)\mathrm{d}t\\
&=C_{k}B(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}) +C_{k}\int_{S-\delta}^{S}\textrm{\rm essup}_{\omega\in\Omega^{0}}\|\nabla_{x}u_{r}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-k}\biggl(\int_{S-\delta}^{r}\frac{1}{\sqrt{r-t}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{t-(S-\delta)}}\mathrm{d}t\biggr)\mathrm{d}r\\
&=C_{k}B(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}) +C_{k}B(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})\int_{S-\delta}^{S}\textrm{\rm essup}_{\omega\in\Omega^{0}}\|\nabla_{x}u_{r}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-k}\mathrm{d}r\\
&\leq C_{k}B(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}) +C_{k}\sqrt{\delta}B(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})\int_{S-\delta}^{S}\frac{1}{\sqrt{t-(S-\delta)}}\textrm{\rm essup}_{\omega\in\Omega^{0}}\|\nabla_{x}u_{r}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-k}\mathrm{d}r,\end{split}

where
B(\cdot,\cdot)
is the Euler Bêta function.
And then,
we get a bound in the left hand-side for
\sqrt{\delta}B(\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}},\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})=\min(1/2,1/{2C_{k}})
.
Choosing
S=t+\delta
in
(
3.11
), we get, for
t\leq T-1
,
a bound for
\textrm{\rm essup}_{\omega\in\Omega^{0}}\|\nabla_{x}u_{t}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-k}
.
∎

3.3
BMO estimates for the minor MFG system

We now fix an

{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-adapted
continuous path
{\bm{X}}^{0}=(X_{t}^{0})_{0\leq t\leq T}
with values in

{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
(not necessarily solving the forward equation in
(
2.11
)). With it,
we associate the
stochastic MFG system
(
2.12
). Since
f
and
g
are assumed to be monotone in Lasry Lions sense, and since we have
an
a priori
estimate for the gradient of the backward component, we can easily do as if the Hamiltonian
were at most of linear growth and hence follow the proof of

[
14
, Theorem 4.3.1]
to get a unique solution to the MFG system. Importantly,
the backward component satisfies the conclusion of
Lemma

3.1

and Proposition

3.3
.
Our first step is to strengthen the estimate provided by
Proposition

3.4
.

Throughout the subsection, we
consider a fixed
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-stopping time
\tau
with values in
[0,T]
and we consider the auxiliary MFG system

(3.12)

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\tilde{\mu}_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}\tilde{\mu}_{t}-{\rm div}_{x}\Bigl(\nabla_{p}H\bigl(\cdot,\nabla_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr)\tilde{\mu}_{t}\Bigr)=0,\quad\ (t,x)\in[\tau,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d},\\
&\partial_{t}\tilde{u}_{t}(x)=-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x)+H\bigl(x,\nabla_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x)\bigr)-F(x,\tilde{\mu}_{t}),\quad(t,x)\in[\tau,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d},\\
&\tilde{u}_{T}(x)=0,\quad x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d},\quad\tilde{\mu}_{\tau}=\mu_{\tau},\end{split}

where
F
is as in Assumption (
B
).
Except for the fact that the initial time is random, this MFG system is deterministic.
Since
F
satisfies the Lasry-Lions monotonicity condition,
the system (
3.12
)
has a unique solution.

Proposition 3.5
.

Under Assumption
(
B
)

and with the the same
notation as above,
there exists a constant
C
,
only depending on the parameters in
(
B
)
except
(\sigma_{0},T)

(in particular,
C
is also independent of
\tau
), such that

{\mathbb{P}}^{0}\biggl(\biggl\{{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{\tau}^{T}\biggl(\mathbb{W}_{1}(\mu_{t},\tilde{\mu}_{t})^{2}+\Bigl\|\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr)-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr)(x)\ \mathrm{d}x\Bigr\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}^{2}\biggr)\,\mathrm{d}t\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}^{0}_{\tau}\biggr]\leq C\biggr\}\biggr)=1.

For simplicity, we assume in the first three steps of the proof that

\tau=0
. We then explain in the very last step how the proof has to be changed when

\tau
is general.

First Step.

By monotonicity of the coefficient
F
and from the standard duality method of mean field games
(see
[
14
, Lemma 3.1.2]
), we have,
for any
t\in[0,T]
,

\begin{split}&-\langle u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t},\mu_{t}-\tilde{\mu}_{t})+c{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{t}^{T}\bigl(\mu_{r}+\tilde{\mu}_{r},|\nabla_{x}(u_{r}-\tilde{u}_{r})|^{2}\bigr)\mathrm{d}r\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{0}\biggr]\\
&\leq-{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{t}^{T}\bigl(f_{r}(X_{r}^{0},\cdot,\mu_{r})-F(\cdot,\mu_{r}),\mu_{r}-\tilde{\mu}_{r}\bigr)\mathrm{d}r+(u_{T}-\tilde{u}_{T},\mu_{T}-\tilde{\mu}_{T})\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{0}\biggr],\end{split}

for a constant
c>0
only depending on the parameters in (
B
) except
(\sigma_{0},T)
.

By boundedness of
u_{T}
and
\tilde{u}_{T}
(but the latter is null), we obtain

(3.13)

\begin{split}&-(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t},\mu_{t}-\tilde{\mu}_{t})+c{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{t}^{T}\bigl(\mu_{r}+\tilde{\mu}_{r},|\nabla_{x}(u_{r}-\tilde{u}_{r})|^{2}\bigr)\mathrm{d}r\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{0}\biggr]\\
&\leq C{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[1+\int_{t}^{T}\sup_{x_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}\|f_{r}(x_{0},\cdot,\mu)-F(\cdot,\mu)\|_{L^{\infty}}\mathrm{d}r\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{0}\biggr].\end{split}

We give below two applications of (
3.13
).

Second Step.

First, we study the equation for
(\mu_{t}-\tilde{\mu}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
. We write

\begin{split}\partial_{t}(\mu_{t}-\tilde{\mu}_{t})&=\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}(\mu_{t}-\tilde{\mu}_{t})+\textrm{\rm div}_{x}\Bigl(\nabla_{p}H\bigl(\cdot,\nabla_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(\cdot)\bigr)(\mu_{t}-\tilde{\mu}_{t})\Bigr)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\textrm{\rm div}_{x}\Bigl(\bigl[\nabla_{p}H\bigl(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot)\bigr)-\nabla_{p}H\bigl(\cdot,\nabla_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(\cdot)\bigr)\bigr]\mu_{t}\Bigr).\end{split}

Following Lemma

6.2
and noticing that
\tilde{\bm{u}}
satisfies the same bounds
as
{\bm{u}}
in Proposition
3.4
, consider now the backward PDE

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}+\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}\varphi_{t}-\nabla_{p}H\bigl(\cdot,\nabla_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(\cdot)\bigr)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t}=0,\quad t\in[0,S];\quad\varphi_{S}=\phi,\end{split}

for a smooth terminal function
\phi
and for some
S\in[0,T]

(since
\tilde{\bm{u}}
is deterministic, so is the above PDE and there is no need
to add an additional martingale term as it would be the case if we were
considering the same equation but driven by
{\bm{u}}
).
By duality, we obtain

\begin{split} \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\bigl(\varphi_{t},\mu_{t}-\tilde{\mu}_{t}\bigr)&=-\Bigl(\bigl[\nabla_{p}H\bigl(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot)\bigr)-\nabla_{p}H\bigl(\cdot,\nabla_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(\cdot)\bigr)\bigr]\mu_{t},\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t}\Bigr).\end{split}

Therefore,
using the fact that
\mu_{0}-\tilde{\mu}_{0}=0
, we obtain

\begin{split}\bigl(\phi,\mu_{S}-\tilde{\mu}_{S}\bigr)&\leq\int_{0}^{S}\|\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t}\|_{L^{\infty}}\bigl(\mu_{t},|\nabla_{p}H\bigl(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot)\bigr)-\nabla_{p}H\bigl(\cdot,\nabla_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(\cdot)\bigr)|^{2}\bigr)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq\|\phi\|_{1}\int_{0}^{S}\exp\bigl(-\gamma(S-t)\bigr)\bigl(\mu_{t},|\nabla_{p}H\bigl(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot)\bigr)-\nabla_{p}H\bigl(\cdot,\nabla_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(\cdot)\bigr)|^{2}\bigr)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}t,\end{split}

with the exponential decay following from
Lemma

6.2
, and for
\gamma
only depending on the parameters
in (
B
) but not on
(\sigma_{0},T)
.
And then,

\begin{split}\|\mu_{S}-\tilde{\mu}_{S}\|_{-1}&\leq\int_{0}^{S}\exp\bigl(-\gamma(S-t)\bigr)\bigl(\mu_{t},|\nabla_{p}H\bigl(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot)\bigr)-\nabla_{p}H\bigl(\cdot,\nabla_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(\cdot)\bigr)|^{2}\bigr)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq\frac{1}{\gamma^{1/2}}\biggl(\int_{0}^{S}\exp\bigl(-\gamma(S-t)\bigr)\bigl(\mu_{t},|\nabla_{p}H\bigl(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot)\bigr)-\nabla_{p}H\bigl(\cdot,\nabla_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(\cdot)\bigr)|^{2}\bigr)\mathrm{d}t\biggr)^{1/2},\end{split}

which yields (replacing
S
by
t
in the left-hand side and
t
by

r
in the right-hand side)

\begin{split}{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{0}^{T}\|\mu_{t}-\tilde{\mu}_{t}\|_{-1}^{2}\mathrm{d}t\biggr]&\leq\frac{1}{\gamma}{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\int_{0}^{T}\biggl(\int_{0}^{t}\exp\bigl(-\gamma(t-r)\bigr)\bigl(\mu_{r},|\nabla_{p}H\bigl(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{r}(\cdot)\bigr)-\nabla_{p}H\bigl(\cdot,\nabla_{x}\tilde{u}_{r}(\cdot)\bigr)|^{2}\bigr)\mathrm{d}r\biggr)\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\frac{1}{\gamma}{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\int_{0}^{T}\bigl(\mu_{r},|\nabla_{p}H\bigl(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{r}(\cdot)\bigr)-\nabla_{p}H\bigl(\cdot,\nabla_{x}\tilde{u}_{r}(\cdot)\bigr)|^{2}\bigr)\biggl(\int_{r}^{T}\exp\bigl(-\gamma(t-r)\bigr)\mathrm{d}t\biggr)\mathrm{d}r\\
&\leq\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\int_{0}^{T}\bigl(\mu_{r},|\nabla_{p}H\bigl(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{r}(\cdot)\bigr)-\nabla_{p}H\bigl(\cdot,\nabla_{x}\tilde{u}_{r}(\cdot)\bigr)|^{2}\bigr)\mathrm{d}r.\end{split}

We now insert (
3.13
) (with
t=0
, recalling that
\mu_{0}=\tilde{\mu}_{0}
)
into the above bound. We obtain (recalling that
\|\mu-\tilde{\mu}\|_{-1}
is the same
as
\mathbb{W}_{1}(\mu,\tilde{\mu})
)

(3.14)

\begin{split}{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{W}_{1}(\mu_{t},\tilde{\mu}_{t})^{2}\mathrm{d}t\biggr]&\leq C\biggl(1+{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\int_{0}^{T}\sup_{x_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}\|f_{r}(x_{0},\cdot,\mu)-F(\cdot,\mu)\|_{\infty}\mathrm{d}r\biggr).\end{split}

Third Step.

We provide a similar estimate for the difference of the two backward components
u-\tilde{u}
. We have

\begin{split} \mathrm{d}_{t}\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr)(x)&=\biggl[-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr)(x)+H\bigl(x,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x)\bigr)-H\bigl(x,\nabla_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x)\bigr)-\bigl(f_{t}(X_{t}^{0},x,\mu_{t})-F(x,\tilde{\mu}_{t})\bigr)\biggr]\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\sigma_{0}v_{t}^{0}(x)\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{0}.\end{split}

Rewriting the difference of the two Hamiltonians as

\begin{split}&H\bigl(x,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x)\bigr)-H\bigl(x,\nabla_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x)\bigr)\\
&=\biggl(\int_{0}^{1}\nabla_{p}H\bigl(x,\theta\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x)+(1-\theta)\nabla_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x)\bigr)\mathrm{d}\theta\biggr)\cdot\bigl(\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x)-\nabla_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x)\bigr),\end{split}

we apply Lemma

6.3
with

(3.15)

b_{t}(x)=-\biggl(\int_{0}^{1}\nabla_{p}H\bigl(x,\theta\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x)+(1-\theta)\nabla_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x)\bigr)\mathrm{d}\theta\biggr).

For a smooth initial condition
q
satisfying
\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}q(x)\mathrm{d}x=0

and for an initial time
t_{0}\in[0,T]
, we solve the Fokker-Planck equation

\partial_{t}q_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}q_{t}+\textrm{\rm div}_{x}\bigl(b_{t}(\cdot)q_{t}\bigr)=0,\quad t\in[t_{0},T]\ ;\quad q_{t_{0}}=q.

We observe that this equation is a random conservative equation, due to the random nature of

(b_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
.
By decomposing
q
in positive and negative parts, it suffices to
construct solutions for
q
a probability density.
Following the proof of Lemma
2.4

and
the first step of the proof of Lemma

2.9
,
we deduce that the solution is measurable with respect to
\omega^{0}
:

(q_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
can be regarded as an
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-progressively measurable process with values in the space of finite signed measures on
{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
.
Then, by duality, we expand
(see
[
14
, Lemma 4.3.11]
)

(3.16)

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}_{t}\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t},q_{t}\bigr)&=-\bigl(f_{t}(X_{t}^{0},\cdot,\mu_{t})-F(\cdot,\tilde{\mu}_{t}),q_{t}\bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}n_{t},\quad t\in[t_{0},T],\end{split}

where
(n_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
is a generic martingale term.
We obtain, for a constant
C
depending on the parameters in (
B
) except
(\sigma_{0},T)
,

\begin{split}\bigl(u_{t_{0}}-\tilde{u}_{t_{0}},q\bigr)&\leq{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\|q_{T}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}\sup_{x_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}\|g(x_{0},\cdot,\mu)\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}+C\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\|q_{t}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}\mathbb{W}_{1}(\mu_{t},\tilde{\mu}_{t})\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\|q_{t}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}\sup_{x_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}\|f_{t}(x_{0},\cdot,\mu)-F(\cdot,\mu)\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}\mathrm{d}t\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}}^{0}\biggr].\end{split}

By Lemma

6.3
(which is stated for deterministic conservative equations but which applies here because

b
is bounded by a deterministic constant), we can find new values of
C
and
\gamma
such that
\|q_{t}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}\leq C\exp(-\gamma(t-t_{0}))\|q\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}
. Therefore,
choosing
q
as

q(\cdot)=(-1)^{l}\partial^{l}\rho(\cdot-x)

and
then
as

q(\cdot)=(-1)^{l}[\partial^{l}\rho(\cdot-x)-\partial^{l}\rho(\cdot-x^{\prime})]
, for
x,x^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
and
\rho
a smooth density
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,
where
\partial^{l}\rho
denotes the derivative of
\rho
along
l
(arbitrary) directions of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
(with possible repetitions),
and then letting
\rho
tend to the Delta mass in
0
, we get

\begin{split}\biggl\|u_{t_{0}}-\tilde{u}_{t_{0}}-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\bigl(u_{t_{0}}-\tilde{u}_{t_{0}}\bigr)(x)\mathrm{d}x\biggr\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}&\leq C\exp(-\gamma(T-t_{0}))+C{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\exp(-\gamma(t-t_{0}))\mathbb{W}_{1}(\mu_{t},\tilde{\mu}_{t})\mathrm{d}t\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}}^{0}\biggr]\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+C\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\exp(-\gamma(t-t_{0}))\sup_{x_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}\|f_{t}(x_{0},\cdot,\mu)-F(\cdot,\mu)\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}\mathrm{d}t.\end{split}

Squaring as done in the second step, we obtain (allowing the constant
C

to vary from line to line as long as it only depends on the various parameters in
(
B
) except
(\sigma_{0},T)
)

\begin{split}\Bigl\|u_{t_{0}}-\tilde{u}_{t_{0}}-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\bigl(u_{t_{0}}-\tilde{u}_{t_{0}}\bigr)(x)\mathrm{d}x\Bigr\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}^{2}&\leq C\exp(-\gamma(T-t_{0}))+C{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\exp(-\gamma(t-t_{0}))\mathbb{W}_{1}(\mu_{t},\tilde{\mu}_{t})^{2}\mathrm{d}t\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}}^{0}\biggr]\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+C\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\exp(-\gamma(t-t_{0}))\sup_{x_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}\|f_{t}(x_{0},\cdot,\mu)-F(\cdot,\mu)\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}\mathrm{d}t,\end{split}

where we used the fact that
f_{t}
and
F
are uniformly bounded. Above,

\gamma
is implicitly hidden in the multiplicative constant
C
. Hence,
by integrating in
t_{0}
and using the same Fubini argument as before, we deduce that

\begin{split}&{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{0}^{T}\Bigl\|\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr)-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr)(x)\ \mathrm{d}x\Bigr\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}^{2}\mathrm{d}t\biggr]\\
&\hskip 15.0pt\leq C+C{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{W}_{1}(\mu_{t},\tilde{\mu}_{t})^{2}\mathrm{d}t\Big]+C\int_{0}^{T}\sup_{x_{0}\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}\|f_{t}(x_{0},\cdot,\mu)-F(\cdot,\mu)\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}\mathrm{d}t.\end{split}

Substituting
the last term on the first line by the upper bound in
(
3.14
), we obtain

\begin{split}{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{0}^{T}\Bigl\|\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr)-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr)(x)\ \mathrm{d}x\Bigr\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}^{2}\mathrm{d}t\biggr]&\leq C+C\int_{0}^{T}\sup_{x_{0}\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}\|f_{t}(x_{0},\cdot,\mu)-F(\cdot,\mu)\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}\mathrm{d}t.\end{split}

The above right-hand side is bounded thanks to (
B2
).

Fourth Step.

Replacing the initial time
0
by a more general stopping time
\tau
with values in

[0,T]
(as given in the assumption of the lemma), we get in a very similar manner (using conditional expectation given
{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}^{0}

instead of expectation) that there exists a constant
C
, independent of

\sigma_{0}
,
\tau
and
T
, such that

\begin{split}{\mathbb{P}}^{0}\biggl(\biggl\{{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{\tau}^{T}\Bigl\|\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr)-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr)(x)\ \mathrm{d}x\Bigr\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}^{2}\mathrm{d}t\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}^{0}_{\tau}\biggr]\leq C\biggr\}\biggr)=1.\end{split}

Reformulating
(
3.14
)
in a similar manner,
we complete the proof.
∎

An important application is (recall
(
3.4
) for the definition of the term
v^{0}
below)

Proposition 3.6
.

Under Assumption
(
B
)
,
there exist an exponent
\gamma>0
and a constant
C
, only depending on the parameters in

(
B
)
except
(\sigma_{0},T)
, such that, for any
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-stopping time
\tau
with values in
[0,T]
,

{\mathbb{P}}^{0}\biggl(\biggl\{{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\exp\biggl(\gamma\sigma_{0}^{2}\int_{\tau}^{T}\Bigl\|v^{0}_{r}(\cdot)-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}v^{0}_{r}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\Bigr\|^{2}_{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)}\ \mathrm{d}r\biggr)\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}^{0}\biggr]\leq C\biggr\}\biggr)=1.

In the sequel, we let

(3.17)

\bar{v}_{t}^{0}(x):=v_{t}^{0}(x)-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}v_{t}^{0}(y)\mathrm{d}y,\quad(t,x)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}.

The proof relies on BMO estimates similar to those used in Section

2
, but there is a subtlety due to the fact that the argument in
the exponential involves a functional norm.

We proceed by Sobolev embeddings. We recall that

{\mathcal{C}}^{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
is included in the Sobolev space

{\mathcal{H}}^{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
.
Also, because

H^{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-1}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
embeds continuously in

{\mathcal{C}}^{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
, we obtain

\begin{split}\bigl\|\bar{v}^{0}_{r}\bigr\|^{2}_{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)}&\leq C\bigl\|\bar{v}^{0}_{r}\bigr\|^{2}_{H^{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-1}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}=C\bigl\|\bar{v}^{0}_{r}\bigr\|^{2}_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-1,2}=C\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}\bigl(1+|k|^{2}\bigr)^{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-1}|(v^{0}_{r},e_{k})|^{2},\end{split}

where we recall
(e_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}
is the standard (complex valued) Fourier basis
of
{\mathbb{T}}^{d}

and
(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes here the standard inner product in
L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
(understood coordinate-wise since

v^{0}
takes values in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
).
Therefore,
for
\tau
as in the statement,

(3.18)

\begin{split}{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{\tau}^{T}\big\|\bar{v}^{0}_{r}\big\|^{2}_{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)}\mathrm{d}r\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}^{0}\biggr]&\leq C\sum_{k\neq 0}\bigl(1+|k|^{2}\bigr)^{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-1}{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{\tau}^{T}|(v^{0}_{r},e_{k})|^{2}\mathrm{d}r\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}^{0}\biggr].\end{split}

Next, we compute the dynamics of the Fourier coefficients. For
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
,

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}_{t}\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t},e_{k}\bigr)&=2\pi^{2}|k|^{2}\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t},e_{k}\bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\bigl(e_{k},H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot))-H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(\cdot))\bigr)\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-\bigl(e_{k},f_{t}(X^{0}_{t},\cdot,\mu_{t})-F(\cdot,\tilde{\mu}_{t})\bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{0}\bigl(v_{t}^{0},e_{k}\bigr)\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{0},\quad t\in[0,T].\end{split}

Taking squared norm, we obtain

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}_{t}|\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t},e_{k}\bigr)|^{2}&\geq 2\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t},e_{k}\bigr)\bigl(e_{k},H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot))-H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(\cdot))\bigr)\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-2\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t},e_{k}\bigr)\bigl(e_{k},f_{t}(X_{t}^{0},\cdot,\mu_{t})-F(\cdot,\tilde{\mu}_{t})\bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{0}^{2}\bigl|\bigl(v_{t}^{0},e_{k}\bigr)\bigr|^{2}\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}n_{t},\end{split}

where
(n_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is a generic martingale term.
Therefore,

\begin{split}&\sigma^{2}_{0}{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{\tau}^{T}\sum_{k\neq 0}\bigl(1+|k|^{2}\bigr)^{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-1}\bigl|\bigl(v_{t}^{0},e_{k}\bigr)\bigr|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}^{0}_{\tau}\biggr]\\
&\leq{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\sum_{k\neq 0}\bigl(1+|k|^{2}\bigr)^{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-1}\bigl|\bigl(u_{T},e_{k}\bigr)\bigr|^{2}\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}^{0}_{\tau}\biggr]\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-2{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{\tau}^{T}\sum_{k\neq 0}\bigl(1+|k|^{2}\bigr)^{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-1}\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t},e_{k}\bigr)\bigl(e_{k},f_{t}(X_{t}^{0},\cdot,\mu_{t})-F(\cdot,\tilde{\mu}_{t})\bigr)\mathrm{d}t\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}^{0}_{\tau}\biggr]\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+2{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{\tau}^{T}\sum_{k\neq 0}\bigl(1+|k|^{2}\bigr)^{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-1}\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t},e_{k}\bigr)\bigl(e_{k},H(\cdot,\nabla u_{t}(\cdot))-H(\cdot,\nabla\tilde{u}_{t}(\cdot))\bigr)\mathrm{d}t\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}^{0}_{\tau}\biggr].\end{split}

And then,
by
(
3.18
) and for a constant
C
as in the statement,

\begin{split}&\sigma_{0}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{\tau}^{T}\big\|\bar{v}^{0}_{r}\big\|^{2}_{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)}\mathrm{d}r\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}^{0}\biggr]\\
&\leq C{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\bigl[\|u_{T}\|^{2}_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-1,2}\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}^{0}_{\tau}\bigr]\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+C{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{\tau}^{T}\Bigl\|\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr)-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr)(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\Bigr\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-1,2}\|f_{t}(X_{t}^{0},\cdot,\mu_{t})-F(\cdot,\tilde{\mu}_{t})\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-1,2}\mathrm{d}t\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}^{0}_{\tau}\biggr]\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+C{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{\tau}^{T}\Bigl\|\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr)-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr)(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\Bigr\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-1,2}\Bigl\|H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot))-H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(\cdot))\Bigr\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-1,2}\mathrm{d}t\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}^{0}_{\tau}\biggr]\\
&\leq C{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\bigl[\|u_{T}\|^{2}_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-1}\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}^{0}_{\tau}\bigr]+C{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{\tau}^{T}\|f_{t}(X_{t}^{0},\cdot,\mu_{t})-F(\cdot,\tilde{\mu}_{t})\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-1}^{2}\mathrm{d}t\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}^{0}_{\tau}\biggr]\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+C{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{\tau}^{T}\Bigl\|\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr)-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr)(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\Bigr\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-1}^{2}\mathrm{d}t\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}^{0}_{\tau}\biggr]\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+C{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{\tau}^{T}\Bigl\|H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot))-H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(\cdot))\Bigr\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-1}^{2}\mathrm{d}t\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}^{0}_{\tau}\biggr].\end{split}

The key point to treat the very last term is to observe that

\begin{split}H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot))-H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(\cdot))=-b_{t}(\cdot)\cdot\bigl(\nabla_{x}u_{t}-\nabla_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr)(\cdot),\end{split}

with
b_{t}(\cdot)
as in
(
3.15
).
By Proposition

3.4
(with a similar result holding true for the derivatives of
\tilde{u}
),
we have a bound for
\|b_{t}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-1}
, independently of
(\sigma_{0},T)
. Thanks to this,

\Bigl\|H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot))-H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(\cdot))\Bigr\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-1}\leq C\bigl\|\nabla_{x}(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t})\bigr\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-1}\leq C\Bigl\|\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr)-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr)(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\Bigr\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}.

And then, using the Lipschitz property of
F
w.r.t.
\mu
in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}
, we obtain

\begin{split}&\sigma_{0}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{\tau}^{T}\big\|\bar{v}^{0}(r,\cdot)\big\|^{2}_{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)}\mathrm{d}r\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}^{0}\biggr]\\
&\leq C\sup_{x_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}\|g(x_{0},\cdot,\mu)\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}+C\int_{\tau}^{T}\sup_{x_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}\|f_{t}(x_{0},\cdot,\mu)-F(\cdot,\mu)\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+C{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{\tau}^{T}\mathbb{W}_{1}(\mu_{t},\tilde{\mu}_{t})^{2}\mathrm{d}t\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}^{0}_{\tau}\biggr]+C{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{\tau}^{T}\Bigl\|\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr)-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\bigl(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr)(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\Bigr\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}^{2}\mathrm{d}t\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}^{0}_{\tau}\biggr],\end{split}

where we removed the square in the
f_{t}-F
term by using boundedness of the latter.
Thanks to
(
B2
).
and Proposition
3.5
, we can bound the
left-hand side by
C
(for a possibly new value of
C
).
The conclusion follows from the theory of BMO martingale, see

[
40
, Theorem 2.2]
.
∎

Remark 3.7
.

The same proof shows that
there exist an exponent
\gamma_{T}>0
and a constant
C_{T}
, possibly depending on
T
, such that, for any
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-stopping time
\tau
with values in
[0,T]
,

{\mathbb{P}}^{0}\biggl(\biggl\{{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\exp\biggl(\gamma_{T}\sigma_{0}^{2}\int_{\tau}^{T}\bigl\|v^{0}_{r}\bigr\|^{2}_{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)}\ \mathrm{d}r\biggr)\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}^{0}\biggr]\leq C_{T}\biggr\}\biggr)=1.

The only difference is that, without the additional centring, we loose the exponential
decay in the second and third steps of the proof of
Proposition

3.5
.

3.4
Forward-backward SDE for the major player

We here provide similar results for the system
(
2.11
)
satisfied by the major player, using now the following auxiliary deterministic HJB equation:

(3.19)

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}w^{0}(t,x_{0})+\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma_{0}^{2}\Delta_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,x_{0})+F^{0}(x_{0})-H^{0}\bigl(x_{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,x_{0})\bigr)=0,\quad(t,x_{0})\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\\
&w^{0}(T,x_{0})=0.\end{split}

Existence and uniqueness of a (classical) solution is standard.
The key point is that, by

[
58
, Theorem 1.20]
, we have a bound for
\sup_{(t,x_{0})\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,x_{0})|
that only depends on the parameters in (
B
),
except
T
.
Using the fact that
F^{0}
has bounded first-order derivatives, we deduce from
standard regularization properties of the
heat kernel that
\sup_{(t,x_{0})\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|\nabla^{2}_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,x_{0})|
is bounded with a bound that only depends on the parameters in (
B
),
except
T
. Notice that when
F^{0}\equiv 0
and
H^{0}(\cdot,0)\equiv 0
, then
w^{0}\equiv 0
.

Lemma 3.8
.

Under Assumption
(
B
)
, for any
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-adapted continuous path

(\mu_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
with values in
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})

and any
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-stopping time
\tau
with values in
[0,T]
, any solution to
the
forward-backward SDE
(
2.11
) (in the sense of item 1 in Definition

2.10
) satisfies

(3.20)

{\mathbb{P}}^{0}\biggl(\biggl\{{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{\tau}^{T}\sigma_{0}^{2}|Z_{r}^{0}-\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(X_{t}^{0})|^{2}\mathrm{d}r\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}^{0}\biggr]\leq C\biggr\}\biggr)=1,

for a constant
C
only depending on the parameters
in

(
B
)

except the parameters
(\sigma_{0},T)
.

Using smoothness of
w^{0}
, we can expand
(Y_{t}^{0}-w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}))_{0\leq t\leq T}
by means of Itô’s formula. We get

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\bigl[Y_{t}^{0}-w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})\bigr]&=-\bigl(f_{t}^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})-F^{0}(X_{t}^{0})\bigr)\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip-25.0pt-\Bigl(L^{0}\bigl(X_{t}^{0},-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})\bigr)+H^{0}\bigl(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})\bigr)-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})\cdot\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip-25.0pt+\sigma_{0}\bigl(Z_{t}^{0}-\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})\bigr)\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{0},\quad t\in[0,T].\end{split}

We recall the standard formula
(see (
2.3
))

(3.21)

H^{0}(x_{0},p_{0})=p_{0}\cdot\nabla_{p}H^{0}(x_{0},p_{0})-L^{0}\bigl(x_{0},-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(x_{0},p_{0})\bigr),

from which we obtain (using
p_{0}=Z_{t}^{0}
)

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}\bigl[Y_{t}^{0}-w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})\bigr]=-\bigl(f_{t}^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})-F^{0}(X_{t}^{0})\bigr)\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-\Bigl(-H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})+H^{0}\bigl(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})\bigr)-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})\cdot\bigl[\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})-Z_{t}^{0}\bigr]\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\sigma_{0}\bigl(Z_{t}^{0}-\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})\bigr)\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{0},\quad t\in[0,T].\end{split}

Here, we write

\begin{split} &H^{0}\bigl(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})\bigr)-H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})\cdot\bigl[\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})-Z_{t}^{0}\bigr]\\
&=\int_{0}^{1}\bigl[\nabla_{p}H^{0}\bigl(X_{t}^{0},\theta\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})+(1-\theta)Z_{t}^{0}\bigr)-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})\bigr]\cdot\bigl(\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})-Z_{t}^{0}\bigr)\mathrm{d}\theta\\
&=\bigl[A_{t}^{0}\bigl(\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})-Z_{t}^{0}\bigr)\bigr]\cdot\bigl(\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})-Z_{t}^{0}\bigr),\end{split}

where we have let

A_{t}^{0}:=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\nabla^{2}_{pp}H^{0}\bigl(X_{t}^{0},\theta\varphi\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})+(1-\varphi\theta)Z_{t}^{0}\bigr)\mathrm{d}\theta\mathrm{d}\varphi.

So, with the notations

P_{t}^{0}:=Y_{t}^{0}-w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}),\quad Q_{t}^{0}:=Z_{t}^{0}-\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}),

we obtain the following BSDE:

\mathrm{d}P_{t}^{0}=\Bigl[-\bigl(f_{t}^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})-F^{0}(X_{t}^{0})\bigr)-(A_{t}^{0}Q_{t}^{0})\cdot Q_{t}^{0}\Bigr]\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{0}Q_{t}^{0}\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{0},\quad t\in[0,T].

Using Girsanov transformation,
it is standard (see

[
9
]
and the references therein about quadratic BSDEs)
to deduce that

(3.22)

\begin{split}|P_{t}^{0}|&\leq\sup_{x_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}|g^{0}(x_{0},\mu)|+\int_{t}^{T}\sup_{x_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}|f_{r}^{0}(x_{0},\mu)-F^{0}(\mu)|\mathrm{d}r\leq C,\end{split}

with
C
independent of
(\sigma_{0},T)
, see (
B2
).

We
now
let
{\bm{E}}^{0}:=(E_{t}^{0}:=\exp(\nu P_{t}^{0}))_{0\leq t\leq T}
, for some parameter
\nu>0
.
Notice from (
3.22
) that we have a global bound for both
{\bm{P}}^{0}
and
{\bm{E}}^{0}
. This bound is independent
of
\sigma_{0}
and
T
(and of
\tau
).
We then expand

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}E_{t}^{0}&=\nu E_{t}^{0}\mathrm{d}P_{t}^{0}+\tfrac{1}{2}\nu^{2}E_{t}^{0}\mathrm{d}\langle P^{0}\rangle_{t}\\
&=\nu E_{t}^{0}\Bigl[-\bigl(f_{t}^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})-F^{0}(X_{t}^{0})\bigr)-(A_{t}^{0}Q_{t}^{0})\cdot Q_{t}^{0}\Bigr]\mathrm{d}t+\tfrac{1}{2}\nu^{2}\sigma_{0}^{2}E_{t}^{0}|Q_{t}^{0}|^{2}\mathrm{d}t+\nu\sigma_{0}E_{t}^{0}Q_{t}^{0}\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{0}.\end{split}

Recall that
\sigma_{0}\geq 1
, choose
\nu\geq 2(\|\nabla^{2}_{pp}H^{0}\|_{\infty}+1)
and deduce that,
for any
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-stopping time
\tau
with values in
[0,T]
,

\begin{split} \sigma_{0}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{\tau}^{T}E_{t}^{0}|Q_{t}^{0}|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}^{0}\biggr]\leq{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[E_{T}^{0}+\int_{\tau}^{T}E_{t}^{0}\bigl|f_{t}^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})-F^{0}(X_{t}^{0})\bigr|\mathrm{d}t\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}^{0}\biggr].\end{split}

Thanks to the bounds for
{\bm{P}}^{0}
and
{\bm{E}}^{0}
and to (
B2
) again,
we obtain

\begin{split} \sigma_{0}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{\tau}^{T}E_{t}^{0}|Q_{t}^{0}|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}^{0}\biggr]&\leq C,\end{split}

with the constant
C
being independent of
\sigma_{0}
and
T
.
∎

Bound
(
3.20
)
is a BMO bound. It ensures that the Doléans-Dade exponential

\begin{split}{\mathcal{E}}_{t}^{0}&:={\mathscr{E}}_{t}\biggl(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\sigma_{0}^{-1}\bigl(\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{r}^{0},Z_{r}^{0})-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{r}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(r,X_{r}^{0}))\bigr)\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{r}^{0}\biggr),\quad t\in[0,T],\end{split}

is a martingale with respect to
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
, see
[
40
, Theorem 2.3]
. In particular, the measure

(3.23)

\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{0}:={\mathcal{E}}_{T}^{0}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}^{0}

is a probability measure. In fact, the very benefit of
Lemma
3.8

is to provide an
L^{1+}
bound for

{\bm{\mathcal{E}}}^{0}
,
independently of
\sigma_{0}
and
T
. Indeed, from

[
40
, Theorem 3.1]
(together with the fact that
\sigma_{0}\geq 1
), we claim

Lemma 3.9
.

Under Assumption
(
B
)
,
there exist two constants
\gamma_{0}>0
and
C_{0}\geq 0
, only depending on the parameters in
(
B
)
except
(\sigma_{0},T)
such that, for any
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-stopping time
\tau
with values in
[0,T]
,

{\mathbb{P}}^{0}\biggl(\biggl\{{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\Bigl[\bigl({\mathcal{E}}_{T}^{0}({\mathcal{E}}_{\tau}^{0})^{-1}\bigr)^{1+\gamma_{0}}\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}^{0}\Bigr]\leq C_{0}\biggr\}\biggr)=1,

and

{\mathbb{P}}^{0}\biggl(\biggl\{{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\exp\biggl(\gamma_{0}\sigma_{0}^{2}\int_{\tau}^{T}|Z_{r}^{0}-\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(r,X^{0}_{r})|^{2}\mathrm{d}r\biggr)\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}^{0}\biggr]\leq C_{0}\biggr\}\biggr)=1.

4
Weak formulation of the major-minor MFG

4.1
Girsanov transformation of the coupled major-minor forward-backward system

The preliminary estimates we obtained in the previous section allow us to make
a change of measure that leads to a new formulation of the major-minor stochastic MFG coupled system,
which we call weak formulation (precisely because it is formulated on a new probability space depending on
