the solution itself). On an arbitrary
filtered probability space

(\Omega^{0},{\mathcal{F}}^{0},{\mathbb{F}}^{0},{\mathbb{P}}^{0})

satisfying the usual conditions and
equipped with a
Brownian motion
(B_{t}^{0})_{0\leq t\leq T}
with values in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, we introduce the tilted Brownian motion

\widetilde{B}_{t}^{0}:=B_{t}^{0}-\sigma_{0}^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}\bigl[\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{r}^{0},Z_{r}^{0})-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{r}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w(r,X_{r}^{0}))\bigr]\mathrm{d}r,\quad t\in[0,T].

Thanks to Lemma
3.8
, we know that
\widetilde{\bm{B}}^{0}=(\widetilde{B}_{t}^{0})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is a Brownian motion under

\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
defined in
(
3.23
), and then
(
2.11
)–(
2.12
) become

(4.1)

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{0}&=-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(X_{t}^{0}))\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{0}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{B}_{t}^{0},\\
\mathrm{d}Y_{t}^{0}&=\Bigl[-f^{0}_{t}(X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})-L^{0}(X_{t}^{0},-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0}))\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+Z_{t}^{0}\cdot\bigl(\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(X_{t}^{0}))\bigr)\Bigr]\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{0}Z_{t}^{0}\cdot\mathrm{d}\widetilde{B}_{t}^{0},\quad t\in[0,T],\\
Y_{T}^{0}&=g^{0}(X_{T}^{0},\mu_{T}),\end{split}

and

(4.2)

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\mu_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}\mu_{t}-{\rm div}_{x}\Bigl(\nabla_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot))\mu_{t}\Bigr)=0,\quad{\rm on}\ (0,T)\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d},\\
&\mathrm{d}_{t}u_{t}(x)=\Bigl(-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}u_{t}(x)+H(x,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x))-f_{t}(X_{t}^{0},x,\mu_{t})\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 20.0pt+\Bigl(\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(X_{t}^{0}))\Bigr)\cdot v_{t}^{0}(x)\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{0}v_{t}^{0}(x)\cdot\mathrm{d}\widetilde{B}_{t}^{0},\quad(t,x)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d},\\
&u_{T}(x)=g(X_{T}^{0},x,\mu_{T}),\quad x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}.\end{split}

This is the system that we use below to get estimates on the values
of the Major/Minor MFG.
The very interest is that the forward equation of the major player is now independent of the remaining three equations.

It is important to observe that the Girsanov transformation (
3.23
) does not impact
the conclusion of Proposition
3.6
: provided we change
\gamma
and
C
in the statement, the bound therein remains true under the tilted measure.
This follows from the fact that,
with
\gamma_{0}
as in Lemma

3.9
,
for any
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-stopping time
\tau
with values in
[0,T]
,

\begin{split}&\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\exp\biggl(\gamma\frac{\gamma_{0}}{1+\gamma_{0}}\sigma_{0}^{2}\int_{\tau}^{T}\bigl\|\bar{v}_{r}^{0}\bigr\|^{2}_{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)}\mathrm{d}r\biggr)\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}^{0}\biggr]\\
&={\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[{\mathcal{E}}_{T}{\mathcal{E}}_{\tau}^{-1}\exp\biggl(\gamma\frac{\gamma_{0}}{1+\gamma_{0}}\sigma_{0}^{2}\int_{\tau}^{T}\bigl\|\bar{v}_{r}^{0}\bigr\|^{2}_{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)}\mathrm{d}r\biggr)\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}^{0}\biggr]\\
&\leq{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\Bigl[\Bigl({\mathcal{E}}_{T}{\mathcal{E}}_{\tau}^{-1}\Bigr)^{1+\gamma_{0}}\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}^{0}\Bigr]^{1/(1+\gamma_{0})}{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\exp\biggl(\gamma\sigma_{0}^{2}\int_{\tau}^{T}\bigl\|\bar{v}_{r}^{0}\bigr\|^{2}_{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)}\mathrm{d}r\biggr)\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}^{0}\biggr]^{\gamma_{0}/(1+\gamma_{0})}\\
&\leq C_{0}^{1/(1+\gamma_{0})}C^{\gamma_{0}/(1+\gamma_{0})},\end{split}

with
C_{0}
as in
Lemma

3.9
.

Letting

\tilde{\gamma}_{0}:=\gamma_{0}\frac{\gamma_{0}}{1+\gamma_{0}},\quad\tilde{\gamma}:=\gamma\frac{\gamma_{0}}{1+\gamma_{0}},\quad\tilde{C}:=C_{0}^{1/(1+\gamma_{0})}C^{\gamma_{0}/(1+\gamma_{0})},

we obtain (proceeding similarly for the second one below), for any
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-stopping time
\tau
with values in
[0,T]
,

(4.3)

\begin{split} &\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\exp\biggl(\tilde{\gamma}\sigma_{0}^{2}\int_{\tau}^{T}\bigl\|\bar{v}^{0}_{r}\bigr\|^{2}_{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)}\mathrm{d}r\biggr)\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}^{0}\biggr]\leq\tilde{C},\\
&\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\exp\biggl(\tilde{\gamma}_{0}\sigma_{0}^{2}\int_{\tau}^{T}|Z_{r}^{0}-\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(X_{r}^{0})|^{2}\mathrm{d}r\biggr)\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}^{0}\biggr]\leq\tilde{C}_{0}.\end{split}

4.2
Strong formulation of the tilted system and related linearized system

We now come to the heart of the paper.
We study the tilted system when posed in the strong form, meaning on the original probability space, with
the given
{\bm{B}}^{0}
as driving Brownian motion.
In clear, we directly address

(4.4)

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{0}=-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(X_{t}^{0}))\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{0}\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{0},\\
&\mathrm{d}Y_{t}^{0}=\Bigl[-f^{0}_{t}(X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})-L^{0}(X_{t}^{0},-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0}))\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+Z_{t}^{0}\cdot\bigl(\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(X_{t}^{0}))\bigr)\Bigr]\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{0}Z_{t}^{0}\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{0},\quad t\in[0,T],\\
&Y_{T}^{0}=g^{0}(X_{T}^{0},\mu_{T}),\end{split}

and

(4.5)

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\mu_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}\mu_{t}-{\rm div}_{x}\Bigl(\nabla_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot))\mu_{t}\Bigr)=0,\quad{\rm on}\ (0,T)\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d},\\
&\mathrm{d}_{t}u_{t}(x)=\Bigl(-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}u_{t}(x)+H(x,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x))-f_{t}(X_{t}^{0},x,\mu_{t})\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 20.0pt+\Bigl(\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(X_{t}^{0}))\Bigr)\cdot v_{t}^{0}(x)\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{0}v_{t}^{0}(x)\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{0},\quad(t,x)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d},\\
&u_{T}(x)=g(X_{T}^{0},x,\mu_{T}),\quad x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d},\end{split}

on the space
(\Omega^{0},{\mathcal{F}}^{0},{\mathbb{F}}^{0},{\mathbb{P}}^{0})
.

The
following statement clarifies the passage from
strong existence and uniqueness for
(
2.11
)–(
2.12
)
to
strong existence and uniqueness for
(
4.4
)–(
4.5
):

Proposition 4.1
.

Under Assumption
(
B
)
,
assume further that, on any arbitrary
probabilistic set-up
(\Omega^{0},{\mathcal{F}}^{0},{\mathbb{F}}^{0},{\mathbb{P}}^{0})
and for a
fixed initial condition
(x_{0},\mu)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
, the system
(
2.11
)–(
2.12
)
has a unique solution in the sense of Definition

2.10
. Assume also that there exist
two bounded and measurable mappings

{\mathcal{U}}^{0}:[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
and

{\mathcal{U}}:[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
, with
{\mathcal{U}}^{0}
and

{\mathcal{U}}
being both
differentiable in the
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
-variable, with

{\mathcal{U}}
being
differentiable in the
{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
-variable
and with

{\mathcal{U}}^{0}
and
\nabla_{x}{\mathcal{U}}
being Lipschitz continuous in the
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and

{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
-variables uniformly in the other variables,
such that,
{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
almost surely,

(4.6)

\begin{split}&Y_{t}^{0}={\mathcal{U}}^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t}),\ Z_{t}^{0}=\nabla_{x_{0}}{\mathcal{U}}^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t}),\quad t\in[0,T],\\
&u_{t}(x)={\mathcal{U}}(t,X_{t}^{0},x,\mu_{t}),\ v_{t}^{0}(x)=\nabla_{x_{0}}{\mathcal{U}}(t,X_{t}^{0},x,\mu_{t}),\quad(t,x)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}.\end{split}

Then,
on the same (and thus on any)
probabilistic set-up
(\Omega^{0},{\mathcal{F}}^{0},{\mathbb{F}}^{0},{\mathbb{P}}^{0})
and for
the same
initial condition
(x_{0},\mu)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
,
the system
(
4.4
)–(
4.5
)
has a unique (hence strong) solution
satisfying the requirements of
Definition

2.10

together with

(4.7)

\sup_{\tau}\biggl\|{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{\tau}^{T}\|v^{0}_{r}\|^{2}_{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)}\mathrm{d}r\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}\biggr]\biggr\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega^{0},{\mathbb{P}}^{0})}<\infty.

Remark 4.2
.

The fact that existence and uniqueness hold true on any

(\Omega^{0},{\mathcal{F}}^{0},{\mathbb{F}}^{0},{\mathbb{P}}^{0})

(equipped with a Brownian motion
{\bm{B}}^{0}
)
implies that
any solution constructed on a sub-filtration of
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
(still carrying
{\bm{B}}^{0}
) must coincide with the solution
constructed on
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
.
In particular, without any loss of generality,

{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
can always be taken as the
filtration generated by
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}^{0}
and

{\bm{B}}^{0}
. In this way, we can recover the setting of Section
3
.

Existence of a (weak) solution
to
(
4.4
)–(
4.5
)
follows from
the aforementioned Girsanov argument (as before, the word ‘weak’ means that the solution is
driven by another Brownian motion, different from the original

{\bm{B}}^{0}
).
This weak solution satisfies
the integrability conditions in
Definition

2.10

because
(ii) and (iii) in item 1 in Definition

2.10

are preserved by BMO changes of measure
(see

[
40
, Theorem 3.3]
)
and (ii) in item 2 is also preserved.
The bound
(
4.7
)
is a consequence of Remark
3.7
.
In fact, the Lipschitz property of
{\mathcal{U}}^{0}
gives here an
L^{\infty}
bound
on
{\bm{Z}}^{0}
that is even stronger than the BMO condition stated in
(iii) in item 1 in Definition
2.10
.

Thanks to (iii) in item 1 of Definition
2.10

(but for
(
4.4
)–(
4.5
)),
the Girsanov transformation can be reverted, hence proving
weak uniqueness.

Strong uniqueness is
proved by means of
(
4.6
).
By weak uniqueness, any weak solution to
(
4.4
)–(
4.5
)
is in fact obtained by changing the Brownian motion
in the original
(
2.11
)–(
2.12
). As such, it must
satisfy
(
4.6
).
Inserting the representation
(
4.6
)
into
the forward equation of
(
4.5
)
(which is the same as the forward equation of
(
2.12
)) and using the fact that

\nabla_{x}{\mathcal{U}}
is Lipschitz in the

{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
-argument, one gets (following the proof of Lemma
2.9
) that the pair

({\bm{X}}^{0},{\bm{\mu}})
is necessarily progressively-adapted to the
(usual augmentation of the)
filtration generated by
{\bm{B}}^{0}
and pathwise unique.
By
(
4.6
),
we deduce that solutions to
(
4.4
)–(
4.5
)
are
progressively-adapted to the
filtration generated by
{\bm{B}}^{0}
and pathwise unique.
∎

Existence and uniqueness of a solution to the system
(
2.11
)–(
2.12
)
(which is the main purpose of this section)
will be eventually established in Subsection
4.6
(using auxiliary results proven in
Section
5
). At this stage, we are given a flow of solutions

\bigl(({\bm{X}}^{0,x_{0},\mu},{\bm{Y}}^{0,x_{0},\mu},{\bm{Z}}^{0,x_{0},\mu}),({\bm{\mu}}^{x_{0},\mu},{\bm{u}}^{x_{0},\mu},{\bm{v}}^{0,x_{0},\mu})\bigr)_{x_{0},\mu}

to the systems
(
4.4
)
and
(
4.5
), parametrized by
(x_{0},\mu)\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})

(
x_{0}
should be understood as
X_{0}^{0,x_{0},\mu}
and
\mu
as
\mu_{0}^{x_{0},\mu}
).
Implicitly,
these solutions are required to satisfy
the same constraints as in Definition

2.10
.

Throughout, we
fix
(x_{0},\mu)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})

and
(x_{0}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
,
and we consider the system satisfied by the following derivatives, for
t\in[0,T]
:

\begin{split}&\bigl(\delta X_{t}^{0},\delta Y_{t}^{0},\delta Z^{0}\bigr)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}_{|\varepsilon=0}\Bigl(X_{t}^{0,x_{0}+\varepsilon(x_{0}^{\prime}-x_{0}),\mu+\varepsilon(\mu^{\prime}-\mu)},Y_{t}^{0,x_{0}+\varepsilon(x_{0}^{\prime}-x_{0}),\mu+\varepsilon(\mu^{\prime}-\mu)},Z_{t}^{0,x_{0}+\varepsilon(x_{0}^{\prime}-x_{0}),\mu+\varepsilon(\mu^{\prime}-\mu)}\Bigr)\\
&\bigl(\delta\mu_{t},\delta u_{t},\delta v_{t}^{0}\bigr)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}_{|\varepsilon=0}\Bigl(\mu_{t}^{x_{0}+\varepsilon(x_{0}^{\prime}-x_{0}),\mu+\varepsilon(\mu^{\prime}-\mu)},u_{t}^{x_{0}+\varepsilon(x_{0}^{\prime}-x_{0}),\mu+\varepsilon(\mu^{\prime}-\mu)},v_{t}^{0,x_{0}+\varepsilon(x_{0}^{\prime}-x_{0}),\mu+\varepsilon(\mu^{\prime}-\mu)}\Bigr).\end{split}

The sense given to the above derivatives will be clarified below (see in particular the proof of
Proposition

4.4
in Subsection
4.7
).
What matters now is the form of the system that is satisfied by
the two triples
(\delta{\bm{X}}^{0},\delta{\bm{Y}}^{0},\delta{\bm{Z}}^{0})

and
(\delta{\bm{\mu}},\delta{\bm{u}},\delta{\bm{v}}^{0})
, which we call
‘linearized’ processes.
Just say that the first triple takes values in

{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}

whilst the second one takes values in a functional
space, namely
{\bm{\nu}}
is Schwartz-distributional valued and

{\bm{u}}
and
{\bm{v}}^{0}
are functional valued.
Throughout, we use the following notation. For a generic (possibly random and say real-valued) function
\Phi
of (some of) the inputs
(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})
,
we denote by

\delta[\Phi(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})]
the term

\delta[\Phi(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})]:=\nabla_{x}\Phi(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})\cdot\delta X_{t}^{0}+\nabla_{z}\Phi(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})\cdot\delta Z_{t}^{0}+(\delta_{\mu}\Phi(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})(\cdot),\delta\mu_{t}).

Then,
using the relationship (see (
2.3
))

\begin{split}&-L^{0}(X_{t}^{0},-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0}))+Z_{t}^{0}\cdot\bigl(\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}))\bigr)\\
&=H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})-\bigl(Z_{t}^{0}-\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})\bigr)\cdot\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}))\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})\cdot\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}))\\
&=\bigl(H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})-H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}))\bigr)-\bigl(Z_{t}^{0}-\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})\bigr)\cdot\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}))\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-L^{0}(X_{t}^{0},-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}))),\end{split}

and noticing that

\begin{split}&\delta\bigl[-H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}))+\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})\cdot\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}))\bigr]\\
&=-\nabla_{x_{0}}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}))\cdot\delta X_{t}^{0}+\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})\cdot\delta\bigl[\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}))\bigr],\end{split}

we obtained, as linearized system for the major player,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\delta X_{t}^{0}=-\delta\bigl[\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}))\bigr]\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\delta Y_{t}^{0}=-\delta\bigl[f_{t}^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})\bigr]\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\hskip 5.0pt+\Bigl[-\bigl(Z_{t}^{0}-\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})\bigr)\cdot\delta\bigl[\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}))\bigr]-\delta\bigl[L^{0}(X_{t}^{0},-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})))\bigr]\Bigr]\mathrm{d}t

(4.8)

\displaystyle\hskip 5.0pt+\bigl(\nabla_{x_{0}}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})-\nabla_{x_{0}}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}))\bigr)\cdot\delta X_{t}^{0}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\hskip 5.0pt+\bigl(\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}))\bigr)\cdot\delta Z_{t}^{0}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\hskip 5.0pt+\sigma_{0}\delta Z_{t}^{0}\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{0},\quad t\in[0,T],

\displaystyle\delta Y_{T}^{0}=\delta\bigl[g^{0}(X_{T}^{0},\mu_{T})\bigr],

with the initial condition
\delta X_{0}=x_{0}^{\prime}-x_{0}
and
where
(X^{0}_{t},Y^{0}_{t},Z_{t}^{0})
is a shorter notation for
(X_{t}^{0,x_{0},\mu},Y_{t}^{0,x_{0},\mu},Z_{t}^{0,x_{0},\mu})
.

Similarly,
the linearized system of the minor player is

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\delta\mu_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}\delta\mu_{t}-{\rm div}_{x}\Bigl(\nabla_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot))\delta\mu_{t}\Bigr)-{\rm div}_{x}\Bigl(\delta\bigl[\nabla_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot))\bigr]\mu_{t}\Bigr)=0,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{t}\delta u_{t}(x)=\Bigl(-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}\delta u_{t}(x)+\nabla_{p}H(x,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x))\cdot\nabla_{x}\delta u_{t}(x)-\delta\bigl[f_{t}(X_{t}^{0},x,\mu_{t})\bigr]\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t

(4.9)

\displaystyle\hskip 15.0pt+\Bigl(\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}))\Bigr)\cdot\delta v_{t}^{0}(x)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\hskip 15.0pt+\delta\Bigl[\Bigl(\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}))\Bigr)\Bigr]\cdot v_{t}^{0}(x)\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{0}\delta v_{t}^{0}(x)\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{0},\quad(t,x)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d},

\displaystyle\delta u_{T}(x)=\delta\bigl[g(X_{T}^{0},x,\mu_{T})\bigr],\quad x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d},

with the initial condition
\delta\mu_{0}=\mu^{\prime}-\mu

and
with the same convention as before that, for
a (say smooth real-valued) function
\Phi(\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x))
of
\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x)
,

\delta\bigl[\Phi(\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x))\bigr]=\nabla\Phi(\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x))\cdot\nabla_{x}\delta u_{t}(x).

Solutions to the two systems (
4.8
)–(
4.9
) are understood in the following sense:

Definition 4.3
.

Let
(({\bm{X}}^{0},{\bm{Y}}^{0},{\bm{Z}}^{0}),({\bm{\mu}},{\bm{u}},{\bm{v}^{0}}))
be a solution to (
4.4
)–(
4.5
) in the sense of Definition
2.10
, for an initial condition
(x_{0},\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.
Then,
for another

(x_{0}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,

1.

Given the initial condition
x_{0}^{\prime}-x_{0}

and an
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-adapted process

{\delta\bm{\mu}}=(\delta\mu_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
with
continuous trajectories in
{\mathcal{C}}^{-\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor+(d/2+1)}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
, we call solution
to
(
4.8
)
any
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-progressively measurable process

({\delta\bm{X}}^{0},{\delta\bm{Y}}^{0},{\delta\bm{Z}}^{0})
with values in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, such that: (i)

\delta{\bm{X}}^{0}
and
\delta{\bm{Y}}^{0}

have continuous trajectories; (ii)
\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}|\delta X_{t}^{0}|\in L^{\infty}(\Omega^{0},\mathbb{P}^{0})
,
\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}|\delta Y_{t}^{0}|\in L^{\infty}(\Omega^{0},\mathbb{P}^{0})
; (iii)
\sup_{\tau}\|{\mathbb{E}}^{0}[\int_{[\tau,T]}|\delta Z_{t}^{0}|^{2}\mathrm{d}t|{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}^{0}]\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega^{0},{\mathbb{P}}^{0})}<\infty
, the supremum being
taken over all stopping times
\tau
;
(iv) the system
(
4.8
) is satisfied
{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
-almost surely.

2.

Given the initial condition
\mu^{\prime}-\mu
, an
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-adapted process

{\delta\bm{X}}^{0}=(\delta X^{0}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
with
\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}|\delta X_{t}^{0}|\in L^{\infty}(\Omega^{0},\mathbb{P}^{0})
and continuous trajectories in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and an
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-progressively measurable process
{\delta\bm{Z}}^{0}=(\delta Z^{0}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
with
\sup_{\tau}\|{\mathbb{E}}^{0}[\int_{[\tau,T]}|\delta Z_{t}^{0}|^{2}\mathrm{d}t|{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}^{0}]\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega^{0},{\mathbb{P}}^{0})}<\infty
, we call solution
to
(
4.9
)
any
Bochner

{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-progressively measurable process

({\delta\bm{\mu}},{\delta\bm{u}},{\delta\bm{v}}^{0})
with values in

{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})\times{\mathcal{C}}^{3+\epsilon}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})\times{\mathcal{C}}^{3+\epsilon}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
for some

\epsilon>0
, such that: (i)

({\delta\bm{\mu}},{\delta\bm{u}})
has continuous trajectories in

{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})\times{\mathcal{C}}^{3+\epsilon}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
;
(ii)
\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|\delta u_{t}\|_{3+\epsilon}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega^{0},{\mathbb{P}}^{0})
,
\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|\delta\mu_{t}\|_{-1}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega^{0},{\mathbb{P}}^{0})
;
(iii)
\mathbb{E}^{0}[\int_{0}^{T}\|\delta v_{t}^{0}\|^{2}_{3+\epsilon}\mathrm{d}t]<\infty
; (iv) the forward equation in (
4.9
) is satisfied
{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
-almost surely in the weak sense; (v) the backward equation in (
4.9
) is satisfied
{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
-almost surely in the classical sense.

3.

For a given initial condition
(x_{0}^{\prime}-x_{0},\mu^{\prime}-\mu)
,
we call a solution to the coupled systems
(
4.8
)–(
4.9
) a pair
(({\delta\bm{X}}^{0},{\delta\bm{Y}}^{0},{\delta\bm{Z}}^{0}),({\delta\bm{\mu}},{\delta\bm{u}},{\delta\bm{v}}^{0}))

satisfying items 1 and 2 right above.

Regarding (iv) in item 2,
\delta{\bm{\mu}}
is said to satisfy (
4.9
)
{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
-almost surely in the weak sense if,

{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
, for any test function function
\varphi:[0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
in the (separable) space of functions that are once differentiable in

t
and three times differentiable in space, with jointly continuous derivatives, it holds, for any

t\in[0,T]
,

(4.10)

\begin{split}&\bigl(\varphi(t,\cdot),\delta\mu_{t}\bigr)-\bigl(\varphi(0,\cdot),\delta\mu_{0}\bigr)=\int_{0}^{t}\bigl(\partial_{t}\varphi(s,\cdot),\delta\mu_{s}\bigr)\mathrm{d}s+\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\bigl(\Delta_{x}\varphi(s,\cdot),\delta\mu_{s}\bigr)\mathrm{d}s\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\int_{0}^{t}\Bigl(\nabla_{x}\varphi(s,\cdot)\cdot\nabla_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{s}(\cdot)),\delta\mu_{s}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\Bigl(\nabla_{x}\varphi(s,\cdot)\cdot\delta\bigl[\nabla_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{s}(\cdot))\bigr],\mu_{s}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

Regarding the solvability of the system
(
4.8
)–(
4.9
), the following statement is proven in Subsection

4.7
:

Proposition 4.4
.

Let Assumption
(
B
)
and the assumptions of Proposition
4.1
hold true (for any initial condition of
the system
(
2.11
)–(
2.12
)). Assume also that
there exist two positive reals
\alpha
and
L

such that, for all
t\in[0,T]
,

{\mathcal{U}}^{0}(t,\cdot,\cdot)\in{\mathscr{D}}^{0}(L,1)

and
{\mathcal{U}}(t,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)\in{\mathscr{D}}(L,1,3+\alpha)
, and
all

(t,x_{0},\mu)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,

(4.11)

\begin{split}&\lim_{h\to 0}\Bigl(\big\|\nabla_{x_{0}}{\mathcal{U}}(t+h,x_{0},\cdot,\mu)-\nabla_{x_{0}}{\mathcal{U}}(t,x_{0},\cdot,\mu)\big\|_{3+\alpha}\\
&+\sup_{l\in\{0,1\}}\sup_{y\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\big\|\nabla_{y}^{l}\delta_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}(t+h,x_{0},\cdot,\mu,\cdot)-\nabla_{y}^{l}\delta_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}(t,x_{0},\cdot,\mu,y)\big\|_{3+\alpha}\Bigr)=0.\end{split}

Let
(x_{0},\mu),(x_{0}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.
Then, on any arbitrary
probabilistic set-up
(\Omega^{0},{\mathcal{F}}^{0},{\mathbb{F}}^{0},{\mathbb{P}}^{0})
, and for the given initial condition
(x_{0}^{\prime}-x_{0},\mu^{\prime}-\mu)
,
the system
(
4.8
)–(
4.9
)
has a (hence strong) solution
(({\delta\bm{X}}^{0},{\delta\bm{Y}}^{0},{\delta\bm{Z}}^{0}),({\delta\bm{\mu}},{\delta\bm{u}},{\delta\bm{v}}^{0}))

satisfying the requirements of
Definition

4.2
and,
{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
almost surely,

(4.12)

\begin{split}&\delta Y_{t}^{0}=\nabla_{x_{0}}{\mathcal{U}}^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})\cdot\delta X_{t}^{0}+\left(\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{U}^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t}),\delta\mu_{t}\right),\quad t\in[0,T],\\
&\delta u_{t}(x)=\nabla_{x_{0}}{\mathcal{U}}(t,X_{t}^{0},x,\mu_{t})\cdot\delta X_{t}^{0}+\left(\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{U}(t,X_{t}^{0},x,\mu_{t}),\delta\mu_{t}\right),\quad(t,x)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}.\end{split}

In the following Subsections

4.3
,

4.4

and

4.5
, the assumptions of Propositions

4.1

and

4.4

are assumed to hold true (in addition to Assumption
(
B
)
)
. Namely,
there exist
two positive reals
\kappa,\alpha>0
and
two mappings

{\mathcal{U}}^{0}:[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
and

{\mathcal{U}}:[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}

in
{\mathscr{D}}^{0}(L,3+\alpha)
and
{\mathscr{D}}(L,1,3+\alpha)
respectively, satisfying the continuity
property
(
4.11
),
such that
the (hence unique) solution to
(
2.11
)–(
2.12
)
satisfies
the representation formula
(
4.6
).

4.3
Tilting the linearized system

The objective is to provide (
a priori
) estimates, for the linearized systems (
4.8
)–(
4.9
), that are independent of
T
. This is the key point in the
study of the solvability of the (double) forward-backward system
(
4.4
)–(
4.5
). Our strategy relies on a new change of measure which we explain now. Indeed, we know (under the standing assumption) that the solution satisfies the analogue of
(
4.3
) but under
{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
. Therefore, we can apply
a new Girsanov transformation (see
[
40
, Theorem 2.3]
). Letting

(4.13)

\begin{split}\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{t}&:={\mathscr{E}}_{t}\biggl(-\sigma_{0}^{-1}\int_{0}^{\cdot}\Bigl(\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{r}^{0},Z_{r}^{0})-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{r}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(r,X_{r}^{0}))\Bigr)\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{r}^{0}\biggr),\quad t\in[0,T],\end{split}

we have that
(\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is an
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-martingale (under

{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
) and we can define the probability measure

(4.14)

\overline{\mathbb{P}}^{0}:=\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{T}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}^{0}.

Then, following the proof of Lemma

3.9
,
and then proceeding as in the derivation of (
4.3
), we deduce

Lemma 4.5
.

Under Assumption
(
B
)
,
there exist constants
\overline{\gamma},\overline{\gamma}_{0}>0
and
\overline{C},\overline{C}_{0}\geq 0
, only depending on
the parameters in
(
B
)
expect
(\sigma_{0},T)
, such that, for any
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-stopping time
\tau
with values in
[0,T]
,

(4.15)

\begin{split} &\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\exp\biggl(\overline{\gamma}\sigma_{0}^{2}\int_{\tau}^{T}\bigl\|\bar{v}_{r}^{0}\bigr\|^{2}_{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)}\mathrm{d}r\biggr)\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}^{0}\biggr]\leq\overline{C},\\
&\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\exp\biggl(\overline{\gamma}_{0}\sigma_{0}^{2}\int_{\tau}^{T}|Z_{r}^{0}-\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(r,X_{r}^{0})|^{2}\mathrm{d}r\biggr)\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}^{0}\biggr]\leq\overline{C}_{0}.\end{split}

Obviously, under the new measure

\overline{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
,
the process

\overline{B}_{t}^{0}:=B_{t}^{0}+\sigma_{0}^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}\Bigl(\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{r}^{0},Z_{r}^{0})-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{r}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(r,X_{r}^{0}))\Bigr)\mathrm{d}r,\quad t\in[0,T],

is an
{\mathbb{F}}^{0}
-Brownian motion and
the forward-backward system (
4.8
)–(
4.9
) writes

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\delta X_{t}^{0}=-\delta\bigl[\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}))\bigr]\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\delta Y_{t}^{0}=-\delta\bigl[f_{t}^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})\bigr]\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\hskip 5.0pt+\Bigl[-\bigl(Z_{t}^{0}-\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})\bigr)\cdot\delta\bigl[\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}))\bigr]-\delta\bigl[L^{0}(X_{t}^{0},-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})))\bigr]\Bigr]\mathrm{d}t

(4.16)

\displaystyle\hskip 5.0pt+\bigl(\nabla_{x_{0}}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})-\nabla_{x_{0}}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}))\bigr)\cdot\delta X_{t}^{0}\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{0}\delta Z_{t}^{0}\cdot\mathrm{d}\overline{B}_{t}^{0},\quad t\in[0,T],

\displaystyle\delta Y_{T}^{0}=\nabla_{x_{0}}g^{0}(X_{T}^{0},\mu_{T})\delta X_{T}^{0}+(\delta_{\mu}g^{0}(X_{T}^{0},\mu_{T}),\delta\mu_{T}),

and

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\delta\mu_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}\delta\mu_{t}-{\rm div}_{x}\Bigl(\nabla_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot))\delta\mu_{t}\Bigr)-{\rm div}_{x}\Bigl(\delta\bigl[\nabla_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot))\bigr]\mu_{t}\Bigr)=0,

(4.17)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{t}\delta u_{t}(x)=\Bigl(-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}\delta u_{t}(x)+\nabla_{p}H(x,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x))\cdot\nabla_{x}\delta u_{t}(x)-\delta\bigl[f_{t}(X_{t}^{0},x,\mu_{t})\bigr]\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\hskip 15.0pt+\delta\Bigl[\Bigl(\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}))\Bigr)\Bigr]\cdot v_{t}^{0}(x)\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{0}\delta v_{t}^{0}(x)\cdot\mathrm{d}\overline{B}_{t}^{0},\quad(t,x)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d},

\displaystyle\delta u_{T}(x)=\nabla_{x_{0}}g(X_{T}^{0},x,\mu_{T})\delta X_{T}^{0}+(\delta_{\mu}g(X_{T}^{0},x,\mu_{T}),\delta\mu_{T}),

where
\left(({\bm{X}}^{0},{\bm{Y}}^{0},{\bm{Z}}^{0}),({\bm{\mu}},{\bm{u}},{\bm{v}^{0}})\right)
solves
(under the mesure
\overline{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
)

(4.18)

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{0}=-\nabla_{p}H^{0}\bigl(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0}\bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{0}\mathrm{d}\overline{B}_{t}^{0},\quad t\in[0,T],\\
&\mathrm{d}Y_{t}^{0}=-\Bigl(f_{t}^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t})+L^{0}\bigl(X_{t}^{0},-\nabla_{p}H^{0}\bigl(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0}\bigr)\bigr)\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{0}Z_{t}^{0}\cdot\mathrm{d}\overline{B}_{t}^{0},\quad t\in[0,T],\\
&X_{0}^{0}=x_{0},\quad Y_{T}^{0}=g^{0}(X_{T}^{0},\mu_{T}).\end{split}

and

(4.19)

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\mu_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}\mu_{t}-{\rm div}_{x}\Bigl(\nabla_{p}H\bigl(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}\bigr)\mu_{t}\Bigr)=0,\quad\ (t,x)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d},\\
&\mathrm{d}_{t}u_{t}(x)=\Bigl(-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}u_{t}(x)+H\bigl(x,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x)\bigr)-f_{t}(X_{t}^{0},x,\mu_{t})\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{0}v_{t}^{0}(x)\cdot\mathrm{d}\overline{B}_{t}^{0},\quad(t,x)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d},\\
&\mu_{0}=\mu,\quad u_{T}(x)=g(X_{T}^{0},x,\mu_{T}),\quad x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}.\end{split}

4.4
A priori estimates for the linearized systems

We now state several standard
a priori
estimates for the linearized systems
(
4.16
)
and
(
4.17
). All of them are borrowed from the
book
[
14
]
.
As a preliminary observation, we notice
from the regularity of
w^{0}

that

Lemma 4.6
.

Under Assumption
(
B
)
,
there exists a constant
C_{T}
, only depending on the parameters in

(
B
)
, such that

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}|\delta X_{t}^{0}|\leq C_{T}|x_{0}-x_{0}^{\prime}|.

This is a straightforward consequence of Gronwall’s lemma.
∎

We continue with the analysis of the backward equation.

Lemma 4.7
.

Under Assumption
(
B
)
,
there exist two constants
C_{T}
and
C
,
only depending on the parameters in

(
B
)

but
with
C
being independent of
\sigma_{0}
and
T
, such that, with probability 1, for any

t\in[0,T]
, and for
\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\in(0,\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)]\setminus{\mathbb{N}}
,

\|\delta u_{t}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\leq C_{T}|x_{0}-x_{0}^{\prime}|+C\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\|\delta\mu_{T}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}+\int_{t}^{T}\Bigl(\|\delta\mu_{r}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}+|\delta Z_{r}^{0}|\,\|\bar{v}_{r}^{0}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}r\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{0}\biggr],

where
we recall the notation

\bar{v}_{t}^{0}=v_{t}^{0}-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}v_{t}^{0}(x)\mathrm{d}x
, see
(
3.17
).

The strategy is inspired from a duality argument developed in the proof of Lemma 4.3.2
in
[
14
]
. It relies on Lemma

6.3
in Appendix (which we already used in the previous section,
see
(
3.15
)
and
(
3.16
)).
For
t_{0}\in[0,T]
and for
q
a smooth (deterministic) function
with
\|q\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\leq 1
, we consider the conservation equation

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}q_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}q_{t}-\textrm{\rm div}_{x}\bigl(\nabla_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot))q_{t}\bigr)=0,\quad t\in[t_{0},t];\quad q_{t_{0}}=q,\end{split}

we compute

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}(\delta u_{t},q_{t})&=-\Bigl(\delta\bigl[f_{t}(X_{t}^{0},x,\mu_{t})\bigr],q_{t}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\delta\Bigl[\Bigl(\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}))\Bigr)\Bigr]\cdot(q_{t},v_{t}^{0})\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\sigma_{0}\bigl(q_{t},\delta v_{t}^{0}\bigr)\cdot\mathrm{d}\overline{B}_{t}^{0},\quad t\in[t_{0},T].\end{split}

And then,
using the regularity properties of
g
and
f

together with Lemmas

4.6

and

6.3
,
we obtain

\begin{split}(\delta u_{t_{0}},q)&=\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[(\delta u_{T},q_{T})+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\Bigl(\delta\bigl[f_{t}(X_{t}^{0},x,\mu_{t})\bigr],q_{t}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 15.0pt -\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\delta\Bigl[\Bigl(\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}))\Bigr)\Bigr]\cdot(q_{t},v_{t}^{0})\,\mathrm{d}t\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}}^{0}\biggr]\\
&\leq C_{T}|x_{0}-x_{0}^{\prime}|+C\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\|\delta\mu_{T}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\Bigl(\|\delta\mu_{t}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}+\bigl(|\delta X_{t}^{0}|+|\delta Z_{t}^{0}|\bigr)\,\|\bar{v}_{t}^{0}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}}^{0}\biggr].\end{split}

By (
4.15
)
and Lemma
4.6

again,
the above bound can be rewritten
(for a new value of the constant
C_{T}
)

\begin{split}(\delta u_{t_{0}},q)&\leq C_{T}|x_{0}-x_{0}^{\prime}|+C\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\|\delta\mu_{T}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\Bigl(\|\delta\mu_{t}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}+|\delta Z_{t}^{0}|\,\|\bar{v}_{t}^{0}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}}^{0}\biggr].\end{split}

For
l\in\{0,\cdots,\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\rfloor\}
,
x
and
h
two elements of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
\rho
a smooth density on

{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, we
observe that, for

q(\cdot)=(-1)^{l}\partial^{l}\rho(\cdot-x)

(with
\partial^{l}\rho
denoting the derivative of
\rho
along
l
arbitrary directions of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
with possible repetitions)
and
any
\varphi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
satisfying

\|\varphi\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\leq 1
,

\bigl|(\varphi,q)\bigr|\leq\Bigl|\nabla^{l}\varphi*\rho(x)\Bigr|\leq 1.

Similarly, for

q(\cdot)=(-1)^{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\rfloor}|h|^{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}+\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\rfloor}[\partial^{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\rfloor}\rho(\cdot-x)-\partial^{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\rfloor}\rho(\cdot-(x+h)]
,

\bigl|(\varphi,q)\bigr|\leq|h|^{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}+\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\rfloor}\Bigl|\nabla^{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\rfloor}\varphi*\rho(x)-\nabla^{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\rfloor}\varphi*\rho(x+h)\Bigr|\leq 1.

And then, denoting by
(\rho_{n})_{n\geq 1}
a sequence of mollifiers on

{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, we deduce that,
\overline{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
-almost surely, for
any
l\in\{0,\cdots,\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\rfloor\}
, any
n\geq 1
and
any
x,h\in{\mathbb{Q}}^{d}
,

\Bigl|\nabla^{l}\Bigl(\delta u_{t_{0}}*\rho_{n}\Bigr)(x)\Bigr|\leq C_{T}|x_{0}-x_{0}^{\prime}|+C\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\|\delta\mu_{T}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\Bigl(\|\delta\mu_{t}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}+|\delta Z_{t}^{0}|\,\|\bar{v}_{t}^{0}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}}^{0}\biggr],

and

\begin{split}&|h|^{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}+\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\rfloor}\Bigl|\nabla^{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\rfloor}\Bigl(\delta u_{t_{0}}*\rho_{n}\Bigr)(x)-\nabla^{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\rfloor}\Bigl(\delta u_{t_{0}}*\rho_{n}\Bigr)(x+h)\Bigr|\\
&\hskip 15.0pt\leq C_{T}|x_{0}-x_{0}^{\prime}|+C\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\|\delta\mu_{T}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\Bigl(\|\delta\mu_{t}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}+|\delta Z_{t}^{0}|\,\|\bar{v}_{t}^{0}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}}^{0}\biggr].\end{split}

Obviously,
this holds true
almost surely, for
any
l\in\{0,\cdots,\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\rfloor\}
, any
n\geq 1
and
any
x,h\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. And since

\delta u_{t_{0}}
is already known to be in

{\mathcal{C}}^{3+\epsilon}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
(see
Proposition

4.4
), we deduce that it belongs to

{\mathcal{C}}^{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})

with

\|\delta u_{t_{0}}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\leq C_{T}|x_{0}-x_{0}^{\prime}|+C\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\|\delta\mu_{T}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\Bigl(\|\delta\mu_{t}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}+|\delta Z_{t}^{0}|\,\|v_{t}^{0}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}}^{0}\biggr].

Here, we also recall that
\delta{\bm{u}}
has continuous values in

{\mathcal{C}}^{3+\epsilon}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
. We deduce that the above is true, almost surely, for any
t_{0}\in[0,T]
.
∎

Lemma 4.8
.

Under Assumption

(
B
)
, there exist an exponent
\gamma>0
and a constant
C
,
only depending on the parameters in

(
B
)

except
\sigma_{0}
and
T
, such that, with probability 1, for any

t\in[0,T]
, for any

\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\in[1,\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)]\setminus{\mathbb{N}}
,

\|\delta\mu_{t}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\leq C\biggl[\|\delta\mu_{0}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}+\int_{0}^{t}\exp(-\gamma(t-r))\Bigl\|\nabla_{x}\delta u_{r}\,\mu_{r}\Bigr\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}+1}\mathrm{d}r\biggr].

In particular,

\|\delta\mu_{t}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}^{2}\leq C\biggl[\|\delta\mu_{0}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\exp(-\gamma(t-r))\bigl(|\nabla_{x}\delta u_{r}|^{2},\mu_{r}\bigr)\mathrm{d}r\biggr],

for a possibly new value of
C
.

We fix
t_{0}\in[0,T]
.
We use again a duality argument by computing
\mathrm{d}_{t}(\delta\mu_{t},\varphi_{t})
, where

(\varphi_{t})_{0\leq t\leq t_{0}}
solves the
(random) backward equation

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta\varphi_{t}+\nabla_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot))\cdot\nabla\varphi_{t},\quad t\in[0,t_{0}];\quad\varphi_{t_{0}}=\phi,\end{split}

where
\phi
is a deterministic function in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
.
Observe that
(\varphi_{t})_{0\leq t\leq t_{0}}
may be anticipative.
By Lemma

6.2
together with the fact that

\nabla_{x}{\bm{u}}
takes values in

{\mathcal{C}}^{3+\epsilon}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})

for some
\epsilon>0
, we deduce that

(\varphi_{t})_{0\leq t\leq t_{0}}
takes values in

{\mathcal{C}}^{3+\epsilon}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
.
In particular,

(\partial_{t}\varphi_{t})_{0\leq t\leq t_{0}}
takes values in

{\mathcal{C}}^{1+\epsilon}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
. Because

\delta{\bm{\mu}}
takes values in

{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
, this makes it possible to expand the duality product

(\delta\mu_{t},\varphi_{t})_{0\leq t\leq t_{0}}
. We obtain

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}_{t}(\delta\mu_{t},\varphi_{t})&=-\Bigl(\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t},\delta\bigl[\nabla_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot))\bigr]\mu_{t}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t.\end{split}

Then,

\begin{split}(\delta\mu_{t_{0}},\phi)&\leq(\delta\mu_{0},\varphi_{0})+C\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\bigl\|\nabla_{x}\varphi_{t}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}-1}\bigl\|\delta\bigl[\nabla_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot))\bigr]\mu_{t}\bigr\|_{-(\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}-1)}\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq C\|\phi\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\biggl[\|\delta\mu_{0}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}+\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\exp(-\gamma(t_{0}-t))\bigl\|\nabla_{x}\delta u_{t}\mu_{t}\bigr\|_{-(\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}-1)}\mathrm{d}t\biggr],\end{split}

where, to get the last line, we used the identity

\delta\bigl[\nabla_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot))\bigr]=\nabla^{2}_{pp}H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot))\nabla_{x}\delta u_{t}
together with the
bound
\|\nabla^{2}_{pp}H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot))\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}-1}\leq C
,
which follows from
Proposition

3.4
.
Taking the supremum over
\phi
in the unit ball of
{\mathcal{C}}^{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
, we complete
the proof.
∎

4.5
A key functional

We now introduce a key object. For a parameter
A>0
, we call
(e_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
the solution to the backward SDE

(4.20)

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}_{t}e_{t}=\frac{\sigma_{0}^{2}}{A}e_{t}\|\bar{v}_{t}^{0}\|_{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)}^{2}\mathrm{d}t+\ell_{t}\mathrm{d}\overline{B}_{t}^{0},\quad t\in[0,T];\quad e_{T}=1,\end{split}

where
we recall the notation

\bar{v}_{t}^{0}=v_{t}^{0}-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}v_{t}^{0}(x)\mathrm{d}x
, see
(
3.17
).

The solution is given by
the following lemma, which is a straightforward consequence of
(
4.15
):

Lemma 4.9
.

Under Assumption

(
B
)
,
the BSDE
(
4.20
) has a unique solution, which is
given by

e_{t}=\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\exp\biggl(-\frac{\sigma_{0}^{2}}{A}\int_{t}^{T}\|\bar{v}_{r}^{0}\|_{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)}^{2}\mathrm{d}r\biggr)\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{0}\biggr],\quad t\in[0,T].

In particular, there exists a constant
c\in(0,1)
, only depending on the parameters in

(
B
)

except
\sigma_{0}
and
T
, such that,
for
A
large enough (independently of
\sigma_{0}
and
T
),
with probability 1, for all
t\in[0,T]
,

(4.21)

c<e_{t}\leq 1.

For any solution
(e_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
as in
(
4.20
),

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}_{t}\biggl[e_{t}\exp\biggl(-\frac{\sigma_{0}^{2}}{A}\int_{0}^{t}\|\bar{v}_{r}^{0}\|_{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)}^{2}\mathrm{d}r\biggr)\biggr]=e_{t}\exp\biggl(-\frac{\sigma_{0}^{2}}{A}\int_{0}^{t}\|\bar{v}_{r}^{0}\|_{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)}^{2}\mathrm{d}r\biggr)\ell_{t}\mathrm{d}\overline{B}_{t}^{0}.\end{split}

Under the standard conditions that

\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}|e_{t}|^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}|\ell_{t}|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\biggr]<\infty,

we see that the right-hand side in the latter expansion yields a martingale. We deduce that

e_{t}=\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\exp\biggl(-\frac{\sigma_{0}^{2}}{A}\int_{t}^{T}\|\bar{v}_{r}^{0}\|_{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)}^{2}\mathrm{d}r\biggr)\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{0}\biggr],\quad t\in[0,T],

and there is no difficulty for proving that the above right-hand side induces a solution.
In order to prove the estimate (
4.21
) (which is the key point in the statement), we recall that
for any positive random variable
\xi
with positive values

\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\bigl[\xi\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{0}\bigr]\geq\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\bigl[\xi^{-1}\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{0}\bigr]^{-1}.

Choosing

\xi=\exp\biggl(-\frac{\sigma_{0}^{2}}{A}\int_{t}^{T}\|\bar{v}_{r}^{0}\|_{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)}^{2}\mathrm{d}r\biggr),

we complete the proof of the lower bound by using
(
4.15
). Importantly, we notice that the lower bound
c
does not depend on
A

(as soon as the latter one satisfies
1/A\leq\bar{\gamma}
). The upper bound is obvious.
∎

Here is the way we use the process
(e_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
.
To clarify the dependence on the various parameters in
(
B
), we let

\kappa_{H^{0}}:=\|\nabla^{2}_{pp}H^{0}\|_{\infty}/2
and
we call
\lambda_{H}
the largest real such that
\nabla^{2}_{pp}H\geq\lambda_{H}I_{d}
.
We also introduce

\theta_{t}:=\sup_{x_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}\|\delta_{\mu}f_{t}^{0}(x_{0},\mu)(\cdot)\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}+\min\bigl(1,\frac{1}{T}\bigr),\quad\Theta_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{s}\mathrm{d}s,\quad t\in[0,T].

By assumption (see (
B3
)),
\Theta_{T}=\int_{0}^{T}\theta_{t}\mathrm{d}t
is bounded by a constant independent of
T
(and of course of

\sigma_{0}
). And by construction,
\theta_{t}^{-1}\leq\max(1,T)
.
Then,
for three (positive) parameters
\varepsilon_{1}
,
\varepsilon_{2}
and
\varrho
, we next compute

\mathrm{d}_{t}\biggl[\varepsilon_{1}\exp(\Theta_{t})|\delta Y_{t}^{0}|^{2}-(\delta\mu_{t},\delta u_{t})+\varepsilon_{2}e_{t}\biggl(\|\delta\mu_{0}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\Bigl(|\nabla_{x}\delta u_{r}|^{2},\mu_{r}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}r\biggr)\biggr],

for a real
\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\in[1,\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)]\setminus{\mathbb{N}}
,
where we recall
\delta\mu_{0}=\mu-\mu^{\prime}
.

We start with the expansion of

\mathrm{d}_{t}[\varepsilon_{1}\exp(\Theta_{t})|\delta Y_{t}^{0}|^{2}]
.
Back to
(
4.16
), we write

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}_{t}\bigl[\varepsilon_{1}\exp(\Theta_{t})|\delta Y_{t}^{0}|^{2}\bigr]&=\varepsilon_{1}\exp(\Theta_{t})\biggl[\sigma_{0}^{2}|\delta Z_{t}^{0}|^{2}+\theta_{t}|\delta Y_{t}^{0}|^{2}\\
&\hskip-30.0pt+\delta Y_{t}^{0}\Bigl[\Bigl(\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}(1)+\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}(|Z_{t}^{0}-\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})|)\Bigr)\cdot\delta X_{t}^{0}\Bigr]+\delta Y_{t}^{0}\Big(\mathcal{O}_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}(\theta_{t}),\delta\mu_{t}\Big)\biggr]\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}n_{t},\end{split}

where
(n_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a generic martingale term, whose precise value may vary from line to line.
For a normed space
(E,\|\cdot\|)
and for a possibly random real
\Delta\geq 0
,

{\mathcal{O}}_{E}(\Delta)
stands for an
E
-valued term satisfying

\|{\mathcal{O}}_{E}(\Delta)\|\leq C|\Delta|
, for a constant
C
that only depends on the
various parameters in (
B
) except

\sigma_{0}
and
T
.

Next, we compute
\mathrm{d}_{t}(\delta\mu_{t},\delta u_{t})
.
By duality, we obtain
from
(
4.17
):

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}_{t}(\delta\mu_{t},\delta u_{t})\\
&=-\Bigl(\delta[\nabla_{x}u_{t}],\delta\bigl[\nabla_{p}H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot))\bigr]\mu_{t}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t-\Bigl(\delta\mu_{t},\delta\bigl[f_{t}(X_{t}^{0},x,\mu_{t})\bigr]\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\delta\Bigl[\Bigl(\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})-\nabla_{p}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0}))\Bigr)\Bigr]\cdot\bigl(v_{t}^{0},\delta\mu_{t}\bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}n_{t}\\
&=\bigg[-\Big((\nabla_{x}\delta u_{t})^{\top}\nabla_{pp}^{2}H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot))\nabla_{x}\delta u_{t},\mu_{t}\Big)-\Big((\delta_{\mu}f_{t}(X_{t}^{0},\cdot,\mu_{t},\cdot),\delta\mu_{t}(\cdot)),\delta\mu_{t}(\cdot)\Big)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(|\delta X_{t}^{0}|\|\delta\mu_{t}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\bigr)+\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\big(|\delta X_{t}^{0}|\bigr)\cdot(v_{t}^{0},\delta\mu_{t})+\bigl(\nabla^{2}_{pp}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})\delta Z_{t}^{0}\bigr)\cdot(v_{t}^{0},\delta\mu_{t})\bigg]\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}n_{t}.\end{split}

Above, we notice that

(v_{t}^{0},\delta\mu_{t})=(\bar{v}_{t}^{0},\delta\mu_{t})
because
the duality bracket

(\delta\mu_{t},{\bm{1}})

(with
{\bm{1}}
standing for the constant function, equal to 1)
is equal to
0
.

Lastly, we handle

\mathrm{d}_{t}[\varepsilon_{2}e_{t}(\|\delta\mu_{0}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}(\nabla_{x}\delta u_{r}|^{2},\mu_{r})\mathrm{d}r)]
. We have, for all
t\in[0,T]
,

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}_{t}\biggl[\varepsilon_{2}e_{t}\biggl(\|\delta\mu_{0}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\bigl(\bigl|\nabla_{x}\delta u_{r}\bigr|^{2},\mu_{r}\bigr)\mathrm{d}r\biggr)\biggr]\\
&=\bigg[\varepsilon_{2}e_{t}\bigl(\bigl|\nabla_{x}\delta u_{t}\bigr|^{2},\mu_{t}\bigr)+\frac{\varepsilon_{2}\sigma_{0}^{2}}{A}e_{t}\|\bar{v}_{t}^{0}\|_{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)}^{2}\biggl(\|\delta\mu_{0}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\bigl(\bigl|\nabla_{x}\delta u_{r}\bigr|^{2},\mu_{r}\bigr)dr\biggr)\bigg]\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}n_{t}.\end{split}

By combining the last three displays, we get

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}_{t}\biggl[\varepsilon_{1}\exp(\Theta_{t})|\delta Y_{t}^{0}|^{2}-(\delta\mu_{t},\delta u_{t})+\varepsilon_{2}e_{t}\biggl(\|\delta\mu_{0}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\bigl(\bigl|\nabla_{x}\delta u_{r}\bigr|^{2},\mu_{r}\bigr)\mathrm{d}r\biggr)\biggr]\\
&=\varepsilon_{1}\exp(\Theta_{t})\biggl[\sigma_{0}^{2}|\delta Z_{t}^{0}|^{2}+\theta_{t}|\delta Y_{t}^{0}|^{2}\biggr]\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\varepsilon_{1}\exp(\Theta_{t})\biggl[\delta Y_{t}^{0}\Bigl[\Bigl(\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}(1)+\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}(|Z_{t}^{0}-\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0})|)\Bigr)\cdot\delta X_{t}^{0}\Bigr]+\delta Y_{t}^{0}\Big(\mathcal{O}_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}(\theta_{t}),\delta\mu_{t}\Big)\biggr]\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\biggl[\Big((\nabla_{x}\delta u_{t})^{\top}\nabla_{pp}^{2}H(\cdot,\nabla_{x}u_{t}(\cdot))\nabla_{x}\delta u_{t},\mu_{t}\Big)+\Big((\delta_{\mu}f_{t}(X_{t}^{0},\cdot,\mu_{t},\cdot),\delta\mu_{t}(\cdot)),\delta\mu_{t}(\cdot)\Big)\biggr]\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\biggl[{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(|\delta X_{t}^{0}|\|\delta\mu_{t}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\bigr)+\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\big(|\delta X_{t}^{0}|\bigr)\cdot(\bar{v}_{t}^{0},\delta\mu_{t})+\bigl(\nabla^{2}_{pp}H^{0}(X_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})\delta Z_{t}^{0}\bigr)\cdot(\bar{v}_{t}^{0},\delta\mu_{t})\biggr]dt\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\bigg[\varepsilon_{2}e_{t}\bigl(\bigl|\nabla_{x}\delta u_{t}\bigr|^{2},\mu_{t}\bigr)+\frac{\varepsilon_{2}\sigma_{0}^{2}}{A}e_{t}\|\bar{v}_{t}^{0}\|_{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)}^{2}\biggl(\|\delta\mu_{0}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\bigl(\bigl|\nabla_{x}\delta u_{r}\bigr|^{2},\mu_{r}\bigr)\biggr)\bigg]\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}n_{t},\end{split}

where
(n_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
stands for a generic martingale term.
All the terms on the second line of the right-hand side are handled by means of Young’s inequality,
in such a way that
the corresponding term

\delta Y_{t}^{0}
is ‘absorbed’ by

\theta_{t}|\delta Y_{t}^{0}|^{2}
(which is greater than
\min(1,T^{-1})|\delta Y_{t}^{0}|^{2}
) on the first line of the right-hand side.
The two terms on the third line of the right-hand side are non-negative: for the first one, this follows from
the convexity of
H
in the variable
p
; for the second one, this follows from the monotonicity of

f
in the variables
(x,\mu)
.
The first two terms on the fourth line of the right-hand side are also handled by means of
Young’s inequality: among the resulting two terms, one of them is
|\delta X_{t}^{0}|^{2}
multiplied by a possibly large constant.
The last term on the fourth line of the right-hand side is handled by a new application of
Young’s inequality, with the term
\delta Z_{t}^{0}
being now ‘absorbed’ by
\varepsilon_{1}\sigma_{0}^{2}|\delta Z_{t}^{0}|^{2}

on the first line.

Then, using
Lemma
4.9

together with
the fact that
\theta_{t}^{-1}\leq\max(1,T)
,
\theta_{t}\leq\kappa+1
and
\Theta_{T}\leq C
, we observe that the above expansion is greater than

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}_{t}\biggl[\varepsilon_{1}\exp(\Theta_{t})|\delta Y_{t}^{0}|^{2}-(\delta\mu_{t},\delta u_{t})+\varepsilon_{2}e_{t}\biggl(\|\delta\mu_{0}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\bigl(\bigl|\nabla_{x}\delta u_{r}\bigr|^{2},\mu_{r}\bigr)\mathrm{d}r\biggr)\biggr]\\
&\geq\biggl[\frac{\varepsilon_{1}\sigma_{0}^{2}}{2}|\delta Z_{t}^{0}|^{2}+\frac{c\varepsilon_{2}\sigma_{0}^{2}}{2A}\bigl\|\bar{v}_{t}^{0}\bigr\|_{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)}^{2}\biggl(\|\delta\mu_{0}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\bigl(\bigl|\nabla_{x}\delta u_{r}\bigr|^{2},\mu_{r}\bigr)\mathrm{d}r\biggr)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-\frac{\kappa_{H^{0}}}{\varepsilon_{1}\sigma_{0}^{2}}\|\delta\mu_{t}\|^{2}_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\bigl\|\bar{v}_{t}^{0}\bigr\|_{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)}^{2}+\lambda_{H}\bigl(|\nabla_{x}\delta u_{t}|^{2},\mu_{t}\bigr)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}|\delta X_{t}^{0}|^{2}-C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}|Z_{t}^{0}-\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(X_{t}^{0})|^{2}|\delta X_{t}^{0}|^{2}-C\varepsilon_{1}\|\delta\mu_{t}\|^{2}_{-\text{{r}}}\biggr]\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}n_{t},\end{split}

for a constant
C
only depending on the parameters in (
B
) except
\sigma_{0}
and
T

and for a constant
C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}
only depending on
\varepsilon_{1}
and the parameters in (
B
) (including
\sigma_{0}
and
T
).
We now recall the upper bound for
\|\delta\mu_{t}\|^{2}_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}
(see Lemma
4.8
).
Allowing the value of
C
to vary from line to line, we obtain

(4.22)

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}_{t}\biggl[\varepsilon_{1}\exp(\theta_{t})|\delta Y_{t}^{0}|^{2}-(\delta\mu_{t},\delta u_{t})+\varepsilon_{2}e_{t}\biggl(\|\delta\mu_{0}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\bigl(\bigl|\nabla_{x}\delta u_{r}\bigr|^{2},\mu_{r}\bigr)\mathrm{d}r\biggr)\biggr]\\
&\geq\biggl[\frac{\varepsilon_{1}\sigma_{0}^{2}}{2}|\delta Z_{t}^{0}|^{2}+\lambda_{H}\bigl(|\nabla_{x}\delta u_{t}|^{2},\mu_{t}\bigr)\\
&\hskip 10.0pt+\biggl\{\Bigl(\frac{c\varepsilon_{2}\sigma_{0}^{2}}{2A}-\frac{C\kappa_{H^{0}}}{\varepsilon_{1}\sigma_{0}^{2}}\Bigr)\bigl\|\bar{v}_{t}^{0}\bigr\|_{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)}^{2}-C\varepsilon_{1}\biggr\}\biggl(\|\delta\mu_{0}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\exp(-\gamma(t-r))\bigl(\bigl|\nabla_{x}\delta u_{r}\bigr|^{2},\mu_{r}\bigr)\mathrm{d}r\biggr)\\
&\hskip 10.0pt-C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}|\delta X_{t}^{0}|^{2}-C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}|Z_{t}^{0}-\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(X_{t}^{0})|^{2}|\delta X_{t}^{0}|^{2}\biggr]\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}n_{t},\end{split}

We deduce the following main inequality:

Proposition 4.10
.

Under Assumption
(
B
)
,
there exist a constant
C\geq 1
only depending
on the parameters in
(
B
)
except
(\sigma_{0},T)

and a constant
C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}
only depending on

\varepsilon_{1}
and
the parameters in
(
B
)
(including
(\sigma_{0},T)
)
and non-decreasing with
T
,
such that,
with the two notations

\kappa_{H^{0}}:=\|\nabla^{2}_{pp}H^{0}\|_{\infty}/2
and

\lambda_{H}:=\sup\{\theta:\nabla^{2}_{pp}H\geq\theta I_{d}\}
, for

A
and

c
as in Lemma

4.9
,
and
under the following two conditions:

i.

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}<\frac{\lambda_{H}}{C}
,

ii.

\displaystyle\frac{c\varepsilon_{2}\sigma_{0}^{2}}{2A}>\frac{C\kappa_{H^{0}}}{\varepsilon_{1}\sigma_{0}^{2}}
,

it holds, for any

\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\in[1,\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)]\setminus{\mathbb{N}}
,

(4.23)

\begin{split}|\delta Y_{0}^{0}|^{2}+\|\delta u_{0}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}^{2}\leq C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}\Bigl(|x_{0}-x_{0}^{\prime}|^{2}+\|\mu-\mu^{\prime}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}^{2}\Bigr).\end{split}

It is worth pointing out that the constant
C
in the statement may depend on

\lambda_{H}
and
\kappa_{H^{0}}
themselves. This makes the two conditions

i
and
ii

less explicit than might be assumed at first sight. However, we think this formulation is useful for the proof.

Integrating (
4.22
) from
0
to
T
, taking expectation and using
condition
ii
in the statement, we obtain
(for a constant
C
only depending on the parameters in (
B
) except
(\sigma_{0},T)
)

(4.24)

\begin{split}&\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\bigg[\varepsilon_{1}\exp(\Theta_{T})|\delta Y_{T}^{0}|^{2}-(\delta\mu_{T},\delta u_{T})+\varepsilon_{2}e_{T}\biggl(\|\delta\mu_{0}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\bigl(\bigl|\nabla_{x}\delta u_{r}\bigr|^{2},\mu_{r}\bigr)\mathrm{d}r\biggr)\bigg]\\
&\geq\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\bigg\{\varepsilon_{1}|\delta Y_{0}^{0}|^{2}-(\delta\mu_{0},\delta u_{0})+\varepsilon_{2}e_{0}\|\delta\mu_{0}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}^{2}\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\int_{0}^{T}\Bigl[\frac{\varepsilon_{1}\sigma_{0}^{2}}{2}|\delta Z_{t}^{0}|^{2}+\lambda_{H}\bigl(|\nabla_{x}\delta u_{t}|^{2},\mu_{t}\bigr)\Bigr]\mathrm{d}t-C\varepsilon_{1}\int_{0}^{T}\biggl[\bigl(|\nabla_{x}\delta u_{t}|^{2},\mu_{t}\bigr)\int_{t}^{T}\exp(-\gamma(r-t))\mathrm{d}r\biggr]\mathrm{d}t\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}\int_{0}^{T}\Bigl[|\delta X_{t}^{0}|^{2}+|Z_{t}^{0}-\nabla_{x_{0}}w^{0}(X_{t}^{0})|^{2}|\delta X_{t}^{0}|^{2}\Bigr]\mathrm{d}t-C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}\|\delta\mu_{0}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}^{2}\biggr\}.\end{split}

Applying (
4.15
) and Lemma
4.6
, we get
that the whole term on the last line is less
C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}|x_{0}-x_{0}^{\prime}|^{2}
, for a constant

C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}
depending only on the parameters in (
B
)
(including
\sigma_{0}
and
T
)
and on
\varepsilon_{1}
.
Moreover,
by
the monotonicity condition (
A3
) and
Lemma

4.8
,
we have
(for possibly new values of
C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}
and
C
)

(4.25)

\begin{split}&\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\Big[(\delta\mu_{T},\delta u_{T})-\varepsilon_{1}\exp(\Theta_{T})|\delta Y_{T}^{0}|^{2}\Big]\\
&=\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\Big[\delta X_{T}^{0}\cdot(\nabla_{x_{0}}g(X_{T}^{0},\cdot,\mu_{T}),\delta\mu_{T})+\Big((\delta_{\mu}g(X_{T}^{0},\cdot,\mu_{T},\cdot),\delta\mu_{T}(\cdot)),\delta\mu_{T}(\cdot)\Big)\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-\varepsilon_{1}\exp(\Theta_{T})\Big(\nabla_{x_{0}}g^{0}(X_{T}^{0},\mu_{T})\cdot\delta X_{T}^{0}+(\delta_{\mu}g^{0}(X_{T}^{0},\mu_{T}),\delta\mu_{T})\Big)^{2}\Big]\\
&\geq-C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\bigl[|\delta X_{T}^{0}|^{2}\bigr]-C\varepsilon_{1}\exp(\Theta_{T})\|\delta\mu_{T}\|^{2}_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\\
&\geq-C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}|x_{0}-x_{0}^{\prime}|^{2}-C\varepsilon_{1}\biggl(\|\delta\mu_{0}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}^{2}+\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\bigl(\bigl|\nabla_{x}\delta u_{r}\bigr|^{2},\mu_{r}\bigr)\mathrm{d}r\bigg]\biggr).\end{split}

Therefore, by adding (
4.24
) and
(
4.25
),
and by dropping the positive term
\varepsilon_{2}e_{0}\|\delta\mu_{0}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}^{2}
, we obtain
(for a new value of
C
)

(4.26)

\begin{split}&\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\bigg[\bigl(\lambda_{H}-C(\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2})\bigr)\int_{0}^{T}\bigl(\bigl|\nabla_{x}\delta u_{r}\bigr|^{2},\mu_{r}\bigr)dr+\frac{\varepsilon_{1}\sigma_{0}^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{T}|\delta Z_{r}^{0}|^{2}dr\bigg]\\
&\leq\biggl[C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}(|x_{0}-x_{0}^{\prime}|^{2}+\|\delta\mu_{0}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}^{2})-\varepsilon_{1}|\delta Y_{0}^{0}|^{2}+(\delta u_{0},\delta\mu_{0})\biggr].\end{split}

Next, we recall
Lemmas

4.7

and

4.8
,
and use (
4.15
) to derive
that

\begin{split}\|\delta u_{0}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}&\leq C_{T}|x_{0}-x_{0}^{\prime}|+C\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\|\delta\mu_{T}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}+\int_{0}^{T}\Bigl(\|\delta\mu_{r}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}+|\delta Z_{r}^{0}|\|\bar{v}_{r}^{0}\|_{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}r\biggr]\\
&\leq C_{T}\bigl(|x_{0}-x_{0}^{\prime}|+\|\delta\mu_{0}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\bigr)+C\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{0}^{T}\bigl(\bigl|\nabla_{x}\delta u_{r}\bigr|^{2},\mu_{r}\bigr)\mathrm{d}r\biggr]^{1/2}+C\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{0}^{T}|\delta Z_{r}^{0}|^{2}\mathrm{d}r\biggr]^{1/2},\end{split}

Squaring it, and applying assumption
i
in the statement
together with (
4.26
),
we obtain

\begin{split}\|\delta u_{0}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}^{2}&\leq C_{T}\big(|x_{0}-x_{0}^{\prime}|^{2}+\|\delta\mu_{0}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}^{2}\big)+C\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\bigl(\bigl|\nabla_{x}\delta u_{r}\bigr|^{2},\mu_{r}\bigr)\mathrm{d}r\bigg]+C\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{0}\biggl[\int_{0}^{T}|\delta Z_{r}^{0}|^{2}\mathrm{d}r\biggr]\\
&\leq C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}\Bigl(|x_{0}-x_{0}^{\prime}|^{2}+\|\delta\mu_{0}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}^{2}+\bigl|(\delta u_{0},\delta\mu_{0})\bigr|\Bigr)-\frac{1}{C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}}|\delta Y_{0}^{0}|^{2},\end{split}

where we assumed without any loss of generality that

C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}\geq 1
.
Rearranging it
(and handling the duality bracket with a Young’s inequality), we complete the proof.
∎

4.6
Application to existence and uniqueness

In this subsection, we NO longer take for granted the assumptions of Propositions

4.1

and

4.4
.

The main objective is to prove
the following statement, which subsumes
Theorem

2.14
:

Theorem 4.11
.

Under Assumption
(
B
)
,
there exist a constant
C\geq 1
, only depending
on the parameters in
(
B
)
except
(\sigma_{0},T)
,
and a constant
C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}
, only depending on

\varepsilon_{1}
and
the parameters in
(
B
)
(including
(\sigma_{0},T)
)
and non-decreasing with
T
,
such that, with the two notations

\kappa_{H^{0}}:=\|\nabla^{2}_{pp}H^{0}\|_{\infty}/2
and

\lambda_{H}:=\sup\{\theta:\nabla^{2}_{pp}H\geq\theta I_{d}\}
, for

A

and
c
as in Lemma

4.9
,
and
under the following two conditions:

i.

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}<\frac{\lambda_{H}}{C}
,

ii.

\displaystyle\frac{c\varepsilon_{2}\sigma_{0}^{2}}{2A}>\frac{C\kappa_{H^{0}}}{\varepsilon_{1}\sigma_{0}^{2}}
,

the following holds true:

a.

for all
(t,x_{0},\mu)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, the system (
2.11
)–(
2.12
) has a unique solution;

b.

there exist
two continuous mappings

{\mathcal{U}}^{0}:[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
and

{\mathcal{U}}:[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}

such that, for any

\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\in[1,\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)]\setminus{\mathbb{N}}
,

{\mathcal{U}}^{0}(t,\cdot,\cdot)

and

{\mathcal{U}}(t,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)

belong to
{\mathscr{C}}^{0}(C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T},\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}})
and

{\mathscr{C}}(C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T},\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}},\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}})

for all

t\in[0,T]

and the representation formula
(
4.6
) holds true;

c.

for a certain
\alpha>0
,
the continuity condition
(
4.11
)
is satisfied and
{\mathcal{U}}^{0}(t,\cdot,\cdot)

and

{\mathcal{U}}(t,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)

belong to
{\mathscr{D}}^{0}(C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T},1)

and

{\mathscr{D}}(C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T},1,3+\alpha)
respectively.

The proof relies on a standard induction argument, used first in
[
26
]
.

First Step.

We prove in Section
5
, see Proposition

5.19

with
R_{0}:=2\max(C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T},\kappa)

and
{\mathbb{s}}:=\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)>3
(since

\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}>d/2+5
)
that, under
Assumptions (
A1
), (
A2
) and (
A4
) (but not
(
A3
)),
there exists a constant
\delta>0
(corresponding to

{\mathfrak{C}}
in
Proposition

5.19
),
depending on the parameters in Assumption
(
A
) but only depending on
g^{0}
and
g
through
any pair of (strictly) positive reals
(\tilde{L},\tilde{\alpha})
such that

g^{0}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{0}(\tilde{L},3+\tilde{\alpha})
and

g\in{\mathscr{C}}(\tilde{L},3+\tilde{\alpha},3+\tilde{\alpha})

(the roles of
\tilde{L}
and
\tilde{\alpha}
in
the statement of Proposition

5.19
are respectively played by

{\mathfrak{L}}
and
{\mathbb{s}}-\lfloor{\mathbb{s}}\rfloor
)
and satisfying
the following variant of
a
,
b
and
c
:

a’.

[see part
iii
in Proposition

5.19
together with the definition of

{\mathbb{r}}
in Theorem
5.1
]
for all
(t_{0},x_{0},\mu)\in[T-\delta,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, the system (
2.11
)–(
2.12
) has a unique solution when: 1.
the BMO borm of

(\int_{0}^{t}{Z}_{s}^{0}\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{s}^{0})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is required to be bounded by
R_{0}
; 2.

{\bm{u}}
is regarded as a continuous process with values in

{\mathcal{C}}^{3+\beta}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
, for a certain
\beta\in(0,\tilde{\alpha})
,
and
the gradient

(\nabla_{x}u_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is also required to be bounded by
R_{0}
; 3. the
stochastic integral
(\int_{t_{0}}^{t}v_{s}^{0}(\cdot)\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{s}^{0})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
is just
regarded as a martingale
(m_{t})_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}
with values in

{\mathcal{C}}^{1+\beta}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
, for the same value of
\beta
;

b’.

[see part
i
in Proposition
5.19

together with the representation formulas
in (
5.26
) and Proposition

5.13
]

there exist two positive reals
L
and
\alpha

(corresponding to
C
and
({\mathbb{s}}+\lfloor{\mathbb{s}}\rfloor)/2

in Proposition
5.19
) and
two continuous mappings

{\mathcal{U}}^{0}:[T-\delta,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
and

{\mathcal{U}}:[T-\delta,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}

such that

{\mathcal{U}}^{0}(t,\cdot,\cdot)

and

{\mathcal{U}}(t,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)

belong to
{\mathscr{D}}^{0}(L,1)
and

{\mathscr{D}}(L,1,3+\alpha)

for all

t\in[T-\delta,T]
,
and the representation formula
(
4.6
) (restricted to the interval
[T-\delta,T]
and without the representation of
v^{0}
which has not been defined yet) holds true;

c’.

[see part
ii
in Proposition
5.19
]

the continuity condition
(
4.11
)
is satisfied.

We now fix
(t_{0},x_{0},\mu)\in[T-\delta,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
. By Lemma

3.1

(which one can invoke with
{\bm{\mu}}
given by
item
a
’ above),
the pair
({\bm{u}},{\bm{m}})
takes values in

{\mathcal{C}}^{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})\times{\mathcal{C}}^{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}-2}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
.
We can also apply
Lemma
3.2
: it
permits to represent the martingale as a stochastic integral (with

{\bm{v}}^{0}
as integrand).
The combination of the latter two lemmas says that
the solution given above fits
the requirements of Definition

2.10
.
By Proposition
3.4
and Lemma

3.8
, we get bounds
for
|Y_{t_{0}}^{0}|
and
\|u_{t_{0}}\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}

from which we deduce that, for a possibly new value of

C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}
,

|{\mathcal{U}}^{0}(t_{0},x_{0},\mu)|

and

\|{\mathcal{U}}(t_{0},x_{0},\cdot,\mu)\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}}

are
bounded by

C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}
. By
Lemma
5.14
(applied
to

({\bm{U}}^{0},{\bm{V}}^{0})=(u_{t}(x),v_{t}(x))_{t_{0}\leq t\leq T}

and

{\mathcal{V}}^{0}={\mathcal{U}}(\cdot,\cdot,x,\cdot)
for
any fixed
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
),
we get the representation formula
(
4.6
)
for

{\bm{v}}^{0}_{t}(x)
(observing that

{\bm{v}}^{0}_{t}
and
\nabla_{x_{0}}{\mathcal{U}}
are continuous in

x
, we get the formula (
4.6
)
for almost every
(t,\omega^{0})\in[0,T]\times\Omega^{0}
, for all
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
).
By Lemma
3.8
again, we also deduce that, whatever the solution

({\bm{X}}^{0,\prime},{\bm{Y}}^{0,\prime},{\bm{Z}}^{0,\prime},{\bm{\mu}}^{\prime},{\bm{u}}^{\prime},{\bm{m}}^{\prime})

to
(
2.11
)–(
2.12
),
the BMO norm of
(\int_{0}^{t}Z_{s}^{0,\prime}\cdot\mathrm{d}B_{s}^{0})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is bounded by

R_{0}
(again, this may require to modify
C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}
), which proves from
a
’
that
({\bm{X}}^{0},{\bm{Y}}^{0},{\bm{Z}}^{0},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{u}},{\bm{m}})
is the unique solution to
(
2.11
)–(
2.12
).
Furthermore, the assumptions of Propositions

4.1

and

4.4

are satisfied over the interval
[T-\delta,T]
.
In turn, we can invoke
Proposition

4.10

to deduce that
(
4.23
)
(at least, the analogue of it, but on the interval
[T-\delta,T]
) holds true.
We explain below how to use this bound.

We recall that the initial conditions to (
4.8
)–(
4.9
) (which are now initialized from
t_{0}
)
are
\delta X_{0}=x_{0}^{\prime}-x_{0}
and
\delta\mu_{0}=\mu^{\prime}-\mu
. And then, by the representation formula
(
4.12
), we have, for

\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\in[3,\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor-(d/2+1)]\setminus{\mathbb{N}}
(which is possible because
\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}>d/2+5
and then
\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{s}}}\rfloor>d/2+4=(d/2+1)+3
),

(4.27)

\begin{split}&\bigl|\nabla_{x_{0}}{\mathcal{U}}^{0}(t_{0},x_{0},\mu)\cdot(x_{0}^{\prime}-x_{0})+\left(\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{U}^{0}(t_{0},x_{0},\mu),\mu^{\prime}-\mu\right)\bigr|\leq C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}\Bigl(|x_{0}^{\prime}-x_{0}|+\|\mu^{\prime}-\mu\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\Bigr),\\
&\Bigl\|\nabla_{x_{0}}{\mathcal{U}}(t_{0},x_{0},\cdot,\mu)\cdot(x_{0}^{\prime}-x_{0})+\left(\delta_{\mu}\mathcal{U}(t_{0},x_{0},\cdot,\mu),\mu^{\prime}-\mu\right)\Bigr\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\leq C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}\Bigl(|x_{0}^{\prime}-x_{0}|+\|\mu^{\prime}-\mu\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\Bigr).\end{split}

Next,
we focus on
\delta_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}
(which is the most difficult term).
Choosing
x_{0}^{\prime}=x_{0}
,
we rewrite the last inequality as

(4.28)

\biggl\|\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\delta_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}(t_{0},x_{0},\mu,\cdot,y)\mathrm{d}\bigl(\mu^{\prime}-\mu\bigr)(y)\biggr\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\leq C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}\|\mu^{\prime}-\mu\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}.

Assume for a while that for some constant
\gamma>0
,
\mu
is bounded from below by

\gamma\textrm{\rm Leb}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}
(which we denote

\mu\geq\gamma\textrm{\rm Leb}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}
).
For a given smooth even density
\rho

and for
l\in\{1,\cdots,\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\rfloor\}
, consider
the
l
th partial derivative

\partial^{l}\rho
of
\rho
along
l
(arbitrary) directions of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
(with possible repetitions). Then,
for
\epsilon\in{\mathbb{R}}
small enough and
y_{0}\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
fixed,

\mu+\epsilon\partial^{l}\rho(y_{0}-\cdot)\cdot\textrm{\rm Leb}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}
is a probability measure and the above yields:

(4.29)

\biggl\|\nabla^{l}_{y_{0}}\biggl(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\delta_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}(t_{0},x_{0},\mu,\cdot,y)\rho(y_{0}-y)\mathrm{d}y\biggr)\biggr\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\leq C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}\|\nabla^{l}\rho\cdot\textrm{\rm Leb}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\leq C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}.

The case
l=0
can be easily included in the left-hand side by returning back to
(
4.28
), choosing therein
\mu^{\prime}=\epsilon\delta_{y_{0}}+(1-\epsilon)\mu
and then invoking
(
2.1
).

Denoting the integral in the right-hand side of
(
4.29
) by
[\delta_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}(t_{0},x_{0},\mu,\cdot,\cdot)*\rho](y_{0})
(with the convolution acting implicitly on the last argument),
we now address the Hölder regularity of
\nabla_{y}^{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\rfloor}[\delta_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}(t_{0},x_{0},\mu,\cdot,\cdot)*\rho]
in the variable

y
by a similar argument. For a vector
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, we apply
(
4.28
)
to
\mu
(still satisfying
\mu\geq\gamma\textrm{\rm Leb}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}
) and
\mu^{\prime}=\mu+\epsilon[\partial^{l}\rho(\cdot-(y_{0}+z))-\partial^{l}\rho(\cdot-y_{0})]

(for
\epsilon
small enough and with
l=\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\rfloor
). We obtain

(4.30)

\begin{split}&\biggl\|\nabla^{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\rfloor}_{y_{0}}\biggl(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\Bigl[\delta_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}(t_{0},x_{0},\mu,\cdot,y_{0}+z-y)-\delta_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}(t_{0},x_{0},\mu,\cdot,y_{0}-y)\Bigr]\rho(y)\mathrm{d}y\biggr)\biggr\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\\
&\leq C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}\Bigl\|\nabla^{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\rfloor}\bigl[\rho(\cdot+z)-\rho\bigr]\cdot\textrm{\rm Leb}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\Bigr\|_{-\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\leq C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}|z|^{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}-\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\rfloor}.\end{split}

The two inequalities (
4.29
)
and
(
4.30
)
are true for any fixed (smooth) density
\rho
.
Considering a sequence of mollifiers
(\rho_{n})_{n\geq 1}
,
this
shows that, for any
l_{x},l_{y}\in\{0,\cdots,\lfloor r\rfloor\}
,
the functions

(\nabla^{l_{x}}_{x}\nabla^{l_{y}}_{y}[\delta_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}(t_{0},x_{0},\mu,\cdot,\cdot)*\rho_{n}])_{n\geq 1}

are relatively compact
for the topology of uniform convergence on
{\mathbb{T}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
.
Since
{\mathcal{U}}(t,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
belongs to

{\mathscr{D}}(L,1,3+\alpha)

for all
t\in[T-\delta,T]
, we
already know that

\delta_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}(t_{0},x_{0},\mu,\cdot,\cdot)*\rho_{n}

converges to

\delta_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}(t_{0},x_{0},\mu,\cdot,\cdot)

for the topology of uniform convergence on
{\mathbb{T}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
.
We deduce that,
for any
l_{x},l_{y}\in\{0,\cdots,\lfloor r\rfloor\}
,
the derivatives

\nabla^{l_{x}}_{x}\nabla^{l_{y}}_{y}\delta_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}(t_{0},x_{0},\mu,\cdot,\cdot)
exist and
satisfy

(4.31)

\begin{split}&\sup_{l=0,\cdots,\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\rfloor}\Bigl\|\nabla^{l}_{y_{0}}\delta_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}(t_{0},x_{0},\mu,\cdot,y)\Bigr\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\leq C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T},\\
&\Bigl\|\nabla^{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\rfloor}_{y_{0}}\delta_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}(t_{0},x_{0},\mu,\cdot,y_{0}+z)-\nabla^{\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\rfloor}_{y_{0}}\delta_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}(t_{0},x_{0},\mu,\cdot,y_{0})\Bigr)\Bigr\|_{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}}\leq C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}|z|^{\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}-\lfloor\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}\rfloor}.\end{split}

The above holds true under the condition

\mu\geq\gamma{\rm Leb}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}
for some
\gamma>0
.
Also,
thanks to the continuity of

\delta_{\mu}{\mathcal{U}}
in
\mu
(given again by the fact that

{\mathcal{U}}(t,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
belongs to

{\mathscr{D}}(L,1,3+\alpha)

for all
t\in[T-\delta,T]
),
we can prove that (
4.31
) is true for any
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})

by a new approximation argument.
Indeed, for any
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, we can consider

(1-\epsilon)\mu+\mu{\rm Leb}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}
as new probability measure:
(
4.31
) is true for all
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, and we can argue by a new compactness argument. Together with
(
4.27
), this shows that, for any
t_{0}\in[T-\delta,T]
,
the function

{\mathcal{U}}(t_{0},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
satisfies the condition

{\mathscr{C}}(C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T},\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}},\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}})

By the same argument,
{\mathcal{U}}^{0}(t_{0},\cdot,\cdot)
satisfies the condition

{\mathscr{C}}^{0}(C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T},\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}})
.

Second Step.

The next step is to iterate, considering now the problem
(
2.11
)–(
2.12
)
with
{\mathcal{U}}^{0}(T-\delta,\cdot,\cdot)
and

{\mathcal{U}}(T-\delta,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
as new terminal conditions
at time
T-\delta
(in place of
g^{0}
and
g
at time
T
).
By the first step (which does not require the terminal condition
g
to satisfy (
A3
)) and thanks to the lower bound
\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}>3
(without any loss of generality, we can assume

\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}}>3+\tilde{\alpha}
for the same
\tilde{\alpha}
as the one used in the first step),
we can find a new constant
\delta^{\prime}>0
only depending on the parameters in
(
B
) and on the constant
C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T}

(which also depends on the parameters in (
B
)) such that
the items
a
’,
b
’ and
c
’ above are satisfied but on

[T-(\delta+\delta^{\prime}),T-\delta]
in place of
[T-\delta,T]
.
This makes it possible to extend the fields

{\mathcal{U}}^{0}
and
{\mathcal{U}}
to the interval
[T-(\delta+\delta^{\prime}),T]
.
By combining the regularity properties given by item
b
’
on the intervals
[T-(\delta+\delta^{\prime}),T-\delta]
and
[T-\delta,T]
respectively, we deduce that

b
’ holds true on the entire
[T-(\delta+\delta^{\prime}),T]
. Similarly,
we can easily check that
c
’ is satisfied on the entire

[T-(\delta+\delta^{\prime}),T]
.

Now, we observe that any solution given by
a
’ on the interval
[T-(\delta+\delta^{\prime}),T-\delta]
can be extended
to the interval
[T-\delta,T]
by solving the problem addressed in the first step but restarting from
the random initial condition
(X_{T-\delta}^{0},\mu_{T-\delta})
. Solvability of the problem
(
2.11
)–(
2.12
) with a random initial condition is not an issue. The analysis of forward-backward systems with random initial conditions has been well documented in other examples: see for instance

[
26
]
for forward-backward stochastic differential equations in finite dimension and

[
14
, Subsection 5.1]
for a similar analysis in the mean field case. Here, one can use the collection of regular conditional probabilities

({\mathbb{P}}^{0}_{T-\delta}(\omega^{0},\cdot))_{\omega^{0}\in\Omega^{0}}
of

{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
given the
\sigma
-field generated by
(B_{s}^{0})_{T-(\delta+\delta^{\prime})\leq s\leq T-\delta}

in order to reduce the system (initialized from a random initial
condition) to a problem with a deterministic initial condition. Namely,
for an initial condition
(X_{T-\delta}^{0},\mu_{T-\delta})
as above, one can solve the pair of two forward equations:

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{0}=-\nabla_{p}H^{0}\bigl(X_{t}^{0},\nabla_{x_{0}}{\mathcal{U}}^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t}^{0})\bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{0}\mathrm{d}B_{t}^{0},\\
&\partial_{t}\mu_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{x}\mu_{t}-{\rm div}_{x}\Bigl(\nabla_{p}H\bigl(\cdot,\nabla_{x}{\mathcal{U}}(t,X_{t}^{0},\cdot,\mu_{t}^{0})\bigr)\mu_{t}\Bigr)=0,\quad t\in[T-\delta,T].\end{split}

Since
{\mathcal{U}}^{0}\in{\mathscr{D}}^{0}(L,3+\alpha)
and

{\mathcal{U}}\in{\mathscr{D}}(L,1,3+\alpha)
, existence and uniqueness are standard (it suffices to solve a conditional McKean-Vlasov equation, see for instance

[
20
, Chapter 2]
).
Then, defining
(Y_{t}^{0},Z_{t}^{0})_{T-\delta\leq t\leq T}
and

(u_{t},v_{t}^{0})_{T-\delta\leq t\leq T}

as suggested by
(
4.6
), namely letting

\begin{split}&Y_{t}^{0}:={\mathcal{U}}^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t}),\ Z_{t}^{0}:=\nabla_{x_{0}}{\mathcal{U}}^{0}(t,X_{t}^{0},\mu_{t}),\quad t\in[T-\delta,T],\\
&u_{t}(x):={\mathcal{U}}(t,X_{t}^{0},x,\mu_{t}),\ v_{t}^{0}(x):=\nabla_{x_{0}}{\mathcal{U}}(t,X_{t}^{0},x,\mu_{t}),\quad(t,x)\in[T-\delta,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d},\end{split}

we know from Proposition

4.1
that,
\omega^{0}
-
{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
-almost surely, under the
disintegrated probability
{\mathbb{P}}^{0}_{T-\delta}(\omega^{0},\cdot)
,
the two triplets
(X^{0}_{t},Y_{t}^{0},Z^{0}_{t})_{T-\delta\leq t\leq T}
and

(\mu_{t},u_{t},v_{t}^{0})_{T-\delta\leq t\leq T}
that have been hence defined solve the
two forward-backward equations in
(
2.11
)–(
2.12
)
(with the realization

(X_{T-\delta}^{0}(\omega^{0}),\mu_{T-\delta}(\omega^{0}))
as deterministic initial condition).
And then,
by construction of the regular conditional probability,
they solve the systems
(
2.11
)–(
2.12
)
under the original probability

{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
, with

(X_{T-\delta}^{0},\mu_{T-\delta})

as (now random) initial condition.

By combining the solution
originally given by
a
’ on
[T-(\delta+\delta^{\prime}),T-\delta]
with
its extension to
[T-\delta,T]
, we get a solution to
(
2.11
)–(
2.12
) in the sense of
a
’, starting from
(t_{0},x_{0},\mu)
with
t_{0}\in[T-(\delta+\delta^{\prime}),T]
.
This solution is necessarily unique. Indeed, if

(\widetilde{X}^{0}_{t},\widetilde{Y}_{t}^{0},\widetilde{Z}^{0}_{t})_{T-(\delta+\delta^{\prime})\leq t\leq T}
and

(\widetilde{\mu}_{t},\widetilde{u}_{t},\widetilde{v}_{t}^{0})_{T-(\delta+\delta^{\prime})\leq t\leq T}

form another solution with the same initial condition, then it satisfies the system
(
2.11
)–(
2.12
) on
[T-\delta,T]
with

(\widetilde{X}^{0}_{T-\delta},\widetilde{\mu}_{T-\delta})
as (random) initial condition.
In particular, for
{\mathbb{P}}^{0}
-almost every
\omega^{0}
, it satisfies the system
(
2.11
)–(
2.12
) on
[T-\delta,T]
under

{\mathbb{P}}_{T-\delta}^{0}(\omega^{0},\cdot)
,
with the realization

(\widetilde{X}^{0}_{T-\delta}(\omega^{0}),\widetilde{\mu}_{T-\delta}(\omega^{0}))
as deterministic initial condition.
And, then, by the uniqueness property on
[T-\delta,T]
,
this new solution can be represented
as in
(
4.6
)
on the interval
[T-\delta,T]
. In particular,

\widetilde{Y}_{T-\delta}^{0}={\mathcal{U}}^{0}(T-\delta,\widetilde{X}_{T-\delta}^{0},\widetilde{\mu}_{T-\delta})

and

\widetilde{u}_{T-\delta}={\mathcal{U}}(T-\delta,\widetilde{X}_{T-\delta}^{0},\cdot,\widetilde{\mu}_{T-\delta})
.
This says that the triplets

(\widetilde{X}^{0}_{t},\widetilde{Y}_{t}^{0},\widetilde{Z}^{0}_{t})_{T-(\delta+\delta^{\prime})\leq t\leq T-\delta}
and

(\widetilde{\mu}_{t},\widetilde{u}_{t},\widetilde{v}_{t}^{0})_{T-(\delta+\delta^{\prime})\leq t\leq T-\delta}

satisfy the same equations as

(X^{0}_{t},Y_{t}^{0},Z^{0}_{t})_{T-(\delta+\delta^{\prime})\leq t\leq T-\delta}
and

(\mu_{t},u_{t},v_{t}^{0})_{T-(\delta+\delta^{\prime})\leq t\leq T-\delta}

on
[T-(\delta+\delta^{\prime}),T-\delta]
. By (short time) uniqueness on
[T-(\delta+\delta^{\prime}),T-\delta]
, the latter two processes coincide.
Then, they restart from the same states at time
T-\delta
and eventually coincide on the entire
[T-(\delta+\delta^{\prime}),T]
.

In the end, this proves that the three items
a
,
b
and
c
listed in the statement now hold true but on the wider interval
[T-(\delta+\delta^{\prime}),T]
.

Third Step.
The proof now follows the scheme introduced in
[
26
]
. We can restart from
the first step, but replacing
[T-\delta,T]
by
[T-(\delta+\delta^{\prime}),T]
. We get that, for any

t_{0}\in[T-(\delta+\delta^{\prime}),T]
,

{\mathcal{U}}^{0}(t_{0},\cdot,\cdot)

and

{\mathcal{U}}(t_{0},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
satisfy the conditions

{\mathscr{C}}^{0}(C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T},\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}})

and

{\mathscr{C}}(C_{\varepsilon_{1},\sigma_{0},T},\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}},\textrm{\rm\text{{r}}})

respectively. And then, we can repeat the second step and extend the
maps
{\mathcal{U}}^{0}
and
{\mathcal{U}}
to

[T-(\delta+2\delta^{\prime}),T-(\delta+\delta^{\prime})]
for the same
\delta^{\prime}
as in the second step.
